Lemma on logarithmic derivative over directed manifolds
Peiqiang Lin

Abstract
. In this paper, we generalize Ahlfors’ lemma on logarithmic derivative to holomorphic tangent curves of directed projective manifolds intersecting closed subschemes. As a consequence, we obtain Algebro-Geometric Ahlfors’ Lemma on Logarithmic Derivative (AALD for short) and General form of Algebro-Geometric Version of Ahlfors’ Lemma on Logarithmic Derivative (GAALD for short) for holomorphic tangent curves of directed projective manifolds. We also get a transform of AALD and GAALD with respect to a linear system. Finally, we get the Second Main Theorem type results for holomorphic curves as the applications of GAALD and its transform.



0.
Introduction

0.1.
Background

In 1925, Nevanlinna
[
Nev25
]
evolved a theory that carries his name, through his groundbreaking work on meromorphic functions.
It consists of two fundamental pillars: the First Main Theorem (FMT for short) and the Second Main Theorem (SMT for short).

The First Main Theorem can be viewed as the integral version of the fundamental theorem of algebra.
The fundamental theorem of algebra indicates the equality between the degree of polynomial functions and the number of their roots, and FMT extends this concept to meromorphic functions by taking the double integral operator
\displaystyle\int_{0}^{r}\frac{dt}{t}\int_{\bigtriangleup(t)}
(see Section
6.3
for details).
However, a boundary term (known as the proximity function) arises due to the non-compactness of the disk
\bigtriangleup(r)
.
It is evident that the proximity function is always non-negative.
Hence achieving the upper bound of the proximity function is crucial to understand the value distribution of meromorphic functions on non-compact domains
\bigtriangleup(r)
.
The Second Main Theorem, which stands as Nevanlinna’s most profound achievement, addresses this very issue.
It gives an upper bound for the sum of proximity functions for arbitrary finite many values (including the infinity).
Moreover, the upper bound given by SMT also controls the ramification term, which implies that SMT can be viewed as the integral version of Riemann-Hurwitz theorem on non-compact domains
\bigtriangleup(r)
.
Because his work focuses on how functions distribute their values, it is also referred to as value distribution theory.

Afterwards, Cartan
[
Car33
]
extended Nevanlinna theory to linear non-degenerate holomorphic curves in (complex) projective space
\mathbb{P}^{n}
intersecting hyperplanes in general position.
Notice that
\mathbb{P}^{1}\cong\mathbb{C}\cup\{\infty\}
and meromorphic functions can be regarded as holomorphic curves in
\mathbb{P}^{1}
.
Thus Cartan’s work is a natural extension of Nevanlinna theory to higher dimensions.

The key lemma in establishing both Nevanlinna’s and Cartan’s results is known as (Nevanlinna’s) Lemma on Logarithmic Derivative (LLD for short) for meromorphic functions (see also Chapter I, Section 6 of
[
LC90
]
or Section 1.2 of
[
NW14
]
).

Lemma 0.1

(Nevanlinna’s LLD)
.

[
Nev29
]

Let
f\colon\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}\bigcup\{\infty\}
be a non-zero meromorphic function. Then

m\left(r,\frac{f^{\prime}}{f}\right)\leqslant O(\log r+\log T(r,f))

holds for all
r\in\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}
outside a Borel subset
E
of finite Lebesgue measure,
where

m\left(r,\frac{f^{\prime}}{f}\right)=\int_{0}^{2\pi}\!\log^{+}\left|\frac{f^{\prime}}{f}(re^{i\theta})\right|\frac{\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\theta}{2\pi},

and
\log^{+}t\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\log\max\{t,1\}
for any
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
.
Especially, if
f
is a rational function, then

m\left(r,\frac{f^{\prime}}{f}\right)\leqslant O(1)\quad(r\to\infty).

Here
m\left(r,\dfrac{f^{\prime}}{f}\right)=m_{\frac{f^{\prime}}{f}}(r,\infty)
is indeed the proximity function defined in Section
6.2
, if we think of
\dfrac{f^{\prime}}{f}
as a holomorphic curve in
\mathbb{P}^{1}
(more precisely, the analytic extension of
[f^{\prime}:f]
) and
\infty
as the divisor
\{[z_{0}:z_{1}]\mid z_{1}=0\}
in
\mathbb{P}^{1}
.

Following
[
WW38
]
, Ahlfors
[
Ahl41
]
generalized Cartan’s work
[
Car33
]
to the case of intersecting linear subspaces in general position.
Thereupon Ahlfors’ and the related works is called Weyl-Ahlfors theory, or Ahlfors-Weyl theory in some literature (see
[
WW43
]
and
[
Sto83
]
).
In his proof, Ahlfors established an innovative estimate as follows, which is the key to Weyl-Ahlfors theory and a lot of papers on value distribution theory for holomorphic curves in projective manifolds.

Lemma 0.2

(=Lemma
7.2
, Ahlfors’ LLD)
.

Let
H
be a hyperplane of
\mathbb{P}^{n}
, and let
f\colon\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{P}^{n}
be a non-constant holomorphic curve such that
f(\mathbb{C})\not\subset H
.
Then for any
0<\varepsilon<1
, one has

\varepsilon\mathcal{N}\left(\frac{\phi_{1}(H)}{\phi_{0}(H)^{1-\varepsilon}}\Omega_{0},r\right)\leqslant(1+\varepsilon)T_{f}(r,\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(1))+O(1),

where
\phi_{k}(H)
is the
k
-th contact function of
f
for
H
and
\Omega_{0}=f^{\ast}\omega_{0}
is the pullback of the Kähler form
\omega_{0}
associated to the Fubini-Study metric on
\mathbb{P}^{n}
.

Because this lemma plays a similar role as Nevanlinna’s LLD in
[
Nev25
]
, we hereinafter prefer to call it Ahlfors’ Lemma on Logarithmic Derivative (Ahlfors’ LLD for short) for holomorphic curves in projective spaces.

Weyl-Ahlfors theory not only provides a geometric approach to the value distribution theory for holomorphic curves in projective spaces, but also inspires the development of value distribution theory for holomorphic curves with more general domains and targets.
For instance, Stoll
[
Sto53
,
Sto54
]
developed Weyl-Ahlfors theory into the theory of meromorphic maps from parabolic spaces into projective spaces.

The significance of Weyl-Ahlfors theory extends far beyond its original scope, influencing various branches of mathematics, such as differential geometry, hyperbolic geometry, and number theory.
Chern investigated the value distribution of holomorphic curves from a differential geometry perspective and published several papers on this subject.
One of his works, joint with Bott
[
BC65
]
, introduced a “connection” to value distribution theory and established a relation with the characteristic classes.

Inspired by Ahlfors’ LLD, Carlson and Griffiths
[
CG72
]
obtained an analogous estimate for (non-degenerate) equidimensional holomorphic maps from
\mathbb{C}^{n}
to projective manifolds
X
, and established value distribution theory for equidimensional holomorphic maps.
And Griffiths and King
[
GK73
]
extended the theory to the case where
n\geqslant\dim X
.
Furthermore, this result in turn led to a SMT type conjecture for holomorphic curves in projective manifolds
X
.

Conjecture 0.3

(Griffiths’ Second Main Conjecture)
.

[
Gri72
]

Let
X
be a projective manifold, and let
D
be an effective divisor on
X
with possibly simple normal crossings singularities.
Let
f\colon\mathbb{C}\longrightarrow X
be a algebraically non-degenerate holomorphic curve.
For arbitrary small
\varepsilon>0
, the inequality

m_{f}(r,D)+T_{f}(r,K_{X})\leqslant\varepsilon T_{f}(r,A)

holds for all
r\in\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}
outside a Borel subset
E
of finite Lebesgue measure, where
K_{X}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\det T_{X}^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}
is the canonical bundle of
X
and
A
is an ample divisor.

Obviously, the well-known Green-Griffiths conjecture
[
GG80
]
is a special case of Griffiths’ Second Main Conjecture.

Remark 0.4
.

The original statement of Griffiths’ Second Main Conjecture is formulated in terms of Nevanlinna defect.
We reformulate it as a SMT type conjecture so that it is more convenient to compare with Lang’s Second Main Conjecture.
This statement is a bit stronger than the original one, but it is still weaker than Lang’s Second Main Conjecture.

On the other hand, Nochka
[
Noc83
]
introduced Nochka weights, and solved Cartan’s conjecture on linear degenerate holomorphic curves in projective spaces, now called Cartan-Nochka theorem (cf.
[
Che90
]
or Section 2.4 of
[
Fuj93
]
for a simplification of Nochka’s proof).
Afterwards, using the trick of Nochka weights, many SMT type results for non-degenerate holomorphic curves or meromorphic maps were extended to the degenerate case.

Brody’s theorem
[
Bro78
]
says that Brody hyperbolicity and Kobayashi hyperbolicity are equivalent for compact manifolds.
Therefore, value distribution theory for degenerate holomorphic curves offers the best approach to the hyperbolicity question.

In addition, Lang
[
Lan86
]
proposed a conjecture of a higher dimensional version of Mordell’s conjecture (cf.
[
Mor22
]
) on rational points, which is known as Lang’s conjecture.
It states that there are at most finitely many rational points on a Kobayashi hyperbolic algebraic manifold over a number field.

What’s more, Vojta
[
Voj87
]
introduced the correspondence between Nevanlinna theory and Diophantine approximation in the case of one variable, and suggested this correspondence can be extended to several variables.
Hence Lang proposed a stronger version of SMT type conjecture, inspired by a counterpart in Diophantine approximation (see Conjecture 5.1 in Chapter VIII of
[
Lan91
]
).

Conjecture 0.5

(Lang’s Second Main Conjecture)
.

Let
X
and
D
be as in Griffiths’ Second Main Conjecture.
There is a proper closed algebraic subset
Z\subsetneq X
such that for any holomorphic curve
f\colon\mathbb{C}\longrightarrow X
with
f(\mathbb{C})\not\subset Z
, the inequality

m_{f}(r,D)+N_{f,\textnormal{ram}}(r)+T_{f}(r,K_{X})\leqslant O(\log r+\log^{+}T_{f}(r,A))

holds for all
r\in\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}
outside a Borel subset
E
of finite Lebesgue measure,
where
N_{f,\textnormal{ram}}(r)=\mathcal{N}([D_{f,\textnormal{ram}}],r)
, and
D_{f,\textnormal{ram}}
is the ramification divisor of
f
.

In this conjecture, holomorphic curve
f
need not be algebraically non-degenerate.
Instead, it suffices that
f(\mathbb{C})\not\subset Z
for some proper algebraic subset
Z
independent of
f
.
So far, these two conjectures are still open, except for some special cases.

0.2.
Value Distribution Theory over Directed Manifolds

Ochiai
[
Och77
]
revived Bloch’s paper
[
Blo26
]
and established the Bloch-Ochiai Theorem.
In his work, Ochiai proved a lemma on holomorphic differentials, which is an analogue to Nevanlinna’s LLD in Nevanlinna theory.
Shortly afterwards, Noguchi
[
Nog77
]
discovered its relation to Deligne’s logarithmic forms
[
Del71
,
Del74
]
, and established Lemma on Logarithmic Differentials for holomorphic curves in compact Kähler manifolds.
Noguchi’s approach unifies the Bloch-Ochiai Theorem and the classical theorem of Borel.
Moreover, Noguchi
[
Nog85
]
extended Lemma on Logarithmic Differentials to the case of meromorphic maps in projective manifolds.

On the other hand, Kobayashi
[
Kob96
]
proposed another geometric formulation of Nevanlinna’s LLD by Radon transform and SMT for one variable (i.e., Nevanlinna’s original version).
Inspired Kobayashi’s proposal, Yamanoi
[
Yam04
]
established a geometric formulation of Nevanlinna’s LLD for meromorphic maps, using Lemma on Logarithmic Differentials for meromorphic maps.
And he called it Algebro-Geometric Version of Nevanlinna’s Lemma on Logarithmic Derivative and took the initials ANLD for short.
However, Yamanoi’s result does not completely coincide with the proposal in
[
Kob96
]
.
Kobayashi considered the theorems over Demailly-Semple jet towers over directed manifolds
(X,T_{X})
, while Yamanoi constructed the compactification of jet space by defining each fiber at
x
as
\bar{J}_{k}X_{x}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\textnormal{P}(J_{k}X_{x}\times\mathbb{C})
.
Recently, Brotbek
[
Bro17
]
constructed Wronskians on Demailly-Semple jet towers, and gave a simple proof of Kobayashi conjecture (cf.
[
Kob70
,
Kob98
]
) for sufficiently large degree of the hypersurfaces.
Brotbek’s approach is much more simple than Siu’s proof in
[
Siu15
]
, and Wronskians on Demailly-Semple jet towers provides the last missing piece of the puzzle for the implication between Kobayashi’s proposal and Cartan’s (or Ahlfors’) SMT.

More generally, we propose that either Griffiths’s or Lang’s Second Main Conjecture can be extended to the category of directed manifolds.

Conjecture 0.6
.

Let
(X,V)
be a directed projective manifold, and let
D
be an effective divisor
X
with possibly simple normal crossings singularities.
For any algebraically non-degenerate holomorphic curve
f\colon(\mathbb{C},T_{\mathbb{C}})\to(X,V)
, we have

m_{f}(r,D)+T_{f}(r,K_{V})\leqslant S_{f}(r),

where
K_{V}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\det V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}
and
S_{f}(r)
is given by Definition
6.32
.

Conjecture 0.7
.

Let
(X,V)
be a directed projective manifold, and let
D
be an effective divisor
X
with possibly simple normal crossings singularities.
There is a closed algebraic subset
Z\subsetneq X
such that for any holomorphic curve
f\colon(\mathbb{C},T_{\mathbb{C}})\to(X,V)
with
f(\mathbb{C})\not\subset Z
, we have

m_{f}(r,D)+N_{f,\textnormal{ram}}(r)+T_{f}(r,K_{V})\leqslant S_{f}(r).

0.3.
Structure

The paper is organized as follows.

Section
1
gives a brief introduction to the construction of directed varieties, the morphisms between directed varieties, and the blow-up of directed varieties.
In Section
2
, we introduce the concept of the (classic) jet spaces and Demailly-Semple jet towers, which are essential for understanding the higher-order derivatives of holomorphic curves.
And then we recall the Green-Griffiths jet differentials and invariant jet differentials, the latter one has better properties and will play an important role in the whole paper.
Section
3
introduces the Wronskians sections on Demailly-Semple jet towers, and generalizes the Wronskians ideal sheaves with respect to the linear systems.

In Section
4
, we give a brief introduction to logarithmic Demailly-Semple jet towers as a generalization of Demailly-Semple jet towers to the logarithmic case, which is introduced by Dethloff and Lu
[
DL01
]
.
Section
5
constructs the logarithmic Wronskian ideal sheaves on logarithmic Demailly-Semple jet towers as an analogue of the Wronskian ideal sheaves in Section
3
.
Furthermore, we show that the the logarithmic Wronskian ideal sheaves corresponds to a subsheaves of the Wronskian ideal sheaves, which helps us to understand the jets of closed subschemes over directed manifolds.

In Section
6
, we introduce the necessary preliminaries from Nevanlinna theory, including Weil functions, the first main theorem.
And we delve Crofton’s formula for Weil functions and proximity functions, and slightly improve the typical application of Calculus Lemma.

In Section
7
, we recall derived curves (or associated curves) of holomorphic curves in projective spaces, and present Ahlfors’ LLD.
Then using the maps to the Grassmannian, we indicate a way to generalize Ahlfors’ LLD to the case of holomorphic tangent curves of directed projective manifolds.
We also define the jets of closed subschemes over directed manifolds, and obtain Ahlfors’ Lemma on Logarithmic Derivative over Directed Manifolds (ALLDD for short), which is one of the central result of this paper.
In addition, we prove General form of Ahlfors’ Lemma on Logarithmic Derivative over Directed Manifolds (GALLDD for short) as a generalization of ALLDD to the case of several closed subschemes, from which we can obtain SMT type results for holomorphic curves more conveniently.

In Section
8
, we prove Algebro-Geometric Version of Ahlfors’ Lemma on Logarithmic Derivative (AALD for short) and General form of Algebro-Geometric Version of Ahlfors’ Lemma on Logarithmic Derivative (GAALD for short) for holomorphic tangent curves of directed projective manifolds.
What’s more, we give various corollaries of AALD and GAALD.
Finally, Section
9
shows some SMT type results as the applications of GAALD.
For more details on the implication between these lemmas and theorems, we refer to the diagram below.

Ahlfors’ LLD

ALLDD

GALLDD

AALD for
1
-jets

AALD for
1
-jets w.r.t. ​
\mathfrak{S}

GAALD for
1
-jets

GAALD for
1
-jets w.r.t. ​
\mathfrak{S}

AALD

GAALD

GAALD w.r.t. ​
\mathfrak{S}

.

Using the language of Weil functions for closed subschemes of Demailly-Semple jet towers, we generalize and modernize Ahlfors’ LLD for
1
-jets as follows.

Lemma 0.8

(= Lemma
7.15
, ALLDD)
.

Let
(X,V)
be a directed projective manifold, and let
Z
be a closed subscheme of
X
.
Let
f\colon(\mathbb{C},T_{\mathbb{C}})\to(X,V)
be a non-constant holomorphic curve such that
f(\mathbb{C})\not\subset\Supp Z
.
Choose a linear system
\mathfrak{S}\subset|L|
on
X
satisfying that
\mathfrak{S}
separates
1
-jets at every point of
X
and
\mathcal{I}_{Z}=\mathfrak{s}_{\supset Z}
.
Then there exists a constant
c>0
such that the inequality

\varepsilon\mathcal{N}\left(\exp\left(2(1-\varepsilon)\lambda_{Z}\circ f-2\lambda_{Z^{(1)}}\circ f_{[1]}\right)f^{\ast}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{dd}^{c}\varphi_{\mathfrak{S}},r\right)\leqslant cT_{f}(r,L)+O(1)

holds for any
0<\varepsilon<1
, where
\mathcal{N}(\Phi,r)
is defined as in Definition
6.9
for
\Phi\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime(1,1)}(\mathbb{C})
.

For real-valued functions
g,h\in L^{2}(U)
on a subset
U\subset\mathbb{C}
, we define the “inner product” of
g
and
h
with respect to the measure
\gamma_{\mathfrak{S}}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{dd}^{c}|z|^{2}
as
\langle g,h\rangle_{\gamma_{\mathfrak{S}},U}=\int_{U}gh\gamma_{\mathfrak{S}}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{dd}^{c}|z|^{2}
.
Then
\mathcal{N}\left(ghf^{\ast}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{dd}^{c}\varphi_{\mathfrak{S}},r\right)=\int_{0}^{r}\langle g,h\rangle_{\gamma_{\mathfrak{S}},\bigtriangleup(t)}\frac{\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}t}{t}
can be regarded as the growth of the “inner product” of
g
and
h
with respect to the measure
\gamma_{\mathfrak{S}}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{dd}^{c}|z|^{2}(=f^{\ast}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{dd}^{c}\varphi_{\mathfrak{S}})
.
Dividing
\varepsilon
on both sides of the inequality in ALLDD, we see that the left-hand side of the inequality above can be interpreted as the growth of the square of the “
L^{2}
-norm” of
\exp\left((1-\varepsilon)\lambda_{Z}\circ f-\lambda_{Z^{(1)}}\circ f_{[1]}\right)
with respect to the measure
\gamma_{\mathfrak{S}}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{dd}^{c}|z|^{2}
,
and the right-hand side can be interpreted as the growth of the square of the “
L^{2}
-norm” of the constant
\left(\dfrac{c}{\varepsilon}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}
with respect to the same measure.
Observe that
\exp\left(\lambda_{Z}\circ f-\lambda_{Z^{(1)}}\circ f_{[1]}\right)
takes the poles only at the points where
f
comes close to
Z
but
f_{[1]}
does not approach
Z^{(1)}
.

Therefore, ALLDD gives a somewhat upper bound for the growth of the number of points (with multiplicity) where
f
comes close to
Z
but
f_{[1]}
does not approach
Z^{(1)}
in the disk
\bigtriangleup(r)
.
More concretely,
\exp\left((1-\varepsilon)\lambda_{Z}\circ f-\lambda_{Z^{(1)}}\circ f_{[1]}\right)
is an approximating evaluation of the exceptional cases when
f
comes close to
Z
but
f_{[1]}
does not approach
Z^{(1)}
, and the growth of its “
L^{2}
-norm” with respect to the measure
\gamma_{\mathfrak{S}}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{dd}^{c}|z|^{2}
is controlled by the growth of the “
L^{2}
-norm” of the constant.

Moreover, in order to get a better estimation in the case where
f
intersects the closed subschemes with singularities, we generalize Ahlfors’ Lemma on Logarithmic Derivative over Directed Manifolds to the case of several closed subschemes.

Lemma 0.9

(= Lemma
7.16
, GALLDD)
.

Let
(X,V)
be a directed projective manifold, and let
Z_{1},\dots,Z_{q}
be closed subschemes of
X
. Let
f\colon(\mathbb{C},T_{\mathbb{C}})\to(X,V)
be a non-constant holomorphic curve such that
f(\mathbb{C})\not\subset\Supp(Z_{1}+\dots+Z_{q})
. For any linear system
\mathfrak{S}\subset|L|
on
X
satisfying that
\mathfrak{S}
separates
1
-jets at every point of
X
and
\mathcal{I}_{Z}=\mathfrak{s}_{\supset Z}
, there exists a constant
c>0
such that the inequality

\varepsilon\mathcal{N}\left(\exp\left(2(1-\varepsilon)\mathop{\max}_{1\leqslant i\leqslant q}\lambda_{Z_{i}}\circ f-2\mathop{\max}_{1\leqslant i\leqslant q}\lambda_{Z_{i}^{(1)}}\circ f_{[1]}\right)f^{\ast}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{dd}^{c}\varphi_{\mathfrak{S}},r\right)\leqslant cT_{f}(r,L)+O(1)

holds for any
0<\varepsilon<1
.

Taking logarithms on both sides of the inequality in ALLDD, and using the Green-Jensen formula and Calculus Lemma, we conclude AALD for
1
-jets.

Theorem 0.10

(= Theorem
8.1
, AALD for
1
-jets)
.

Let
(X,V)
be a directed projective manifold, and let
Z
be a closed subscheme of
X
.
Let
f\colon(\mathbb{C},T_{\mathbb{C}})\to(X,V)
be a non-constant holomorphic curve such that
f(\mathbb{C})\not\subset\Supp Z
.
Then we have

m_{f}(r,Z)+T_{f_{[1]}}(r,\mathcal{O}_{X_{1}}(1))+\mathcal{N}([Z_{f^{\prime}}],r)\leqslant m_{f_{[1]}}(r,Z^{(1)})+S_{f}(r).

This implies that not only the difference between the proximity functions for
Z
and
Z^{(1)}
but also the sum of the characteristic function for
\mathcal{O}_{X_{1}}(1)
and the counting function for
[Z_{f^{\prime}}]
is controlled by the growth of
S_{f}(r)
.
The controlling of the characteristic function for
\mathcal{O}_{X_{1}}(1)
is essential to deal with the hyperbolicity questions.

Viewing
(X_{k},V_{k})
as the Demailly-Semple
1
-jet tower of
(X_{k-1},V_{k-1})
, we immediately get AALD by induction.

Theorem 0.11

(= Theorem
8.2
, AALD)
.

Let
(X,V)
be a directed projective manifold, and let
Z
be a closed subscheme of
X
.
Let
f\colon(\mathbb{C},T_{\mathbb{C}})\to(X,V)
be a non-constant holomorphic curve such that
f(\mathbb{C})\not\subset\Supp Z
.
For each
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, we have

m_{f_{[k-1]}}(r,Z^{(k-1)})+T_{f_{[k]}}(r,\mathcal{O}_{X_{k}}(1))+\mathcal{N}([Z_{f_{[k-1]}^{\prime}}],r)\leqslant m_{f_{[k]}}(r,Z^{(k)})+S_{f}(r).

Furthermore, in order to prove SMT type results more conveniently, we need the following transform of AALD.

Theorem 0.12

(= Theorem
8.4
, AALD for
1
-jets w.r.t. ​
\mathfrak{S}
)
.

Let
(X,V)
be a directed projective manifold, and let
Z
be a closed subscheme of
X
.
Choose a linear system
\mathfrak{S}\subset|L|
on
X
satisfying that
\mathcal{I}_{Z}=\mathfrak{s}_{\supset Z}
.
Let
f\colon(\mathbb{C},T_{\mathbb{C}})\to(X,V)
be a non-constant holomorphic curve such that
f_{[1]}(\mathbb{C})\not\subset\Bs(\mathfrak{W}(X_{1},\mathfrak{S}))
.
Suppose that
f(\mathbb{C})\not\subset\Supp Z
.
Then,

m_{f}(r,Z)+T_{f_{[1]}}(r,\mathcal{O}_{X_{1}}(1))+\mathcal{N}([Z_{f^{\prime}}],r)\leqslant m_{f_{[1]}}(r,Z_{\mathfrak{S}}^{[1]})-m_{f}(r,Z_{\mathfrak{s}})+S_{f}(r),

where
Z_{\mathfrak{S}}^{[k]}
is the closed subscheme of
X_{k}
with the ideal sheaf
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}(X_{k},Z,\mathfrak{S})
.

Since
Z_{1}^{(1)}+Z_{2}^{(1)}\subsetneqq(Z_{1}+Z_{2})^{(1)}
and
(Z_{1})_{\mathfrak{S}}^{(1)}+(Z_{2})_{\mathfrak{S}}^{(1)}\subsetneqq(Z_{1}+Z_{2})_{\mathfrak{S}}^{(1)}
in general, we refine AALD for
1
-jets and obtain the following two theorems in order to deal with SMT type questions better, especially in the case of intersecting closed subscheme with singularities.

Theorem 0.13

(= Theorem
8.10
, GAALD for
1
-jets)
.

Let
(X,V)
be a directed projective manifold, and let
Z_{1},\dots,Z_{q}
be closed subschemes of
X
.
Take an integer
1\leqslant\ell\leqslant q
.
Let
f\colon(\mathbb{C},T_{\mathbb{C}})\to(X,V)
be a non-constant holomorphic curve such that
f(\mathbb{C})\not\subset\Supp(Z_{1}+\dots+Z_{q})
. Then,

m_{f}(r,\bigvee_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\subset J\\
|I|=\ell\end{subarray}}\sum_{i\in I}Z_{i})+T_{f_{[1]}}(r,\mathcal{O}_{X_{1}}(\ell))+\ell\mathcal{N}([Z_{f^{\prime}}],r)\leqslant m_{f_{[1]}}(r,\bigvee_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\subset J\\
|I|=\ell\end{subarray}}\sum_{i\in I}Z_{i}^{(1)})+S_{f}(r),

where
\displaystyle m_{f}(r,\bigvee_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\subset J\\
|I|=\ell\end{subarray}}\sum_{i\in I}Z_{i})
and
\displaystyle m_{f_{[1]}}(r,\bigvee_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\subset J\\
|I|=\ell\end{subarray}}\sum_{i\in I}Z_{i}^{(1)})
are defined as (
8.10
).

Theorem 0.14

(= Theorem
8.11
, GAALD for
1
-jets w.r.t. ​
\mathfrak{S}
)
.

Let
(X,V)
be a directed projective manifold, and let
Z_{1},\dots,Z_{q}
be closed subschemes of
X
.
Choose a linear system
\mathfrak{S}
on
X
satisfying that
\mathcal{I}_{Z_{i}}=\mathfrak{s}_{\supset Z_{i}}
for each
1\leqslant i\leqslant q
.
Take an integer
1\leqslant\ell\leqslant q
.
Let
f\colon(\mathbb{C},T_{\mathbb{C}})\to(X,V)
be a non-constant holomorphic curve such that
f_{[1]}(\mathbb{C})\not\subset\Bs(\mathfrak{W}(X_{1},\mathfrak{S}))
.
Suppose that
f(\mathbb{C})\not\subset\Supp(Z_{1}+\dots+Z_{q})
. Then we have

\displaystyle m_{f}(r,\!\bigvee_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\subset J\\
|I|=\ell\end{subarray}}\!\sum_{i\in I}Z_{i})+T_{f_{[1]}}(r,\mathcal{O}_{X_{1}}(\ell))+\ell\mathcal{N}([Z_{f^{\prime}}],r)

\displaystyle\leqslant\,

\displaystyle m_{f_{[1]}}\big(r,\!\bigvee_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\subset J\\
|I|=\ell\end{subarray}}\!\sum_{i\in I}(Z_{i})_{\mathfrak{S}}^{[1]}\big)-\ell m_{f}(r,Z_{\mathfrak{s}})+S_{f}(r).

Finally, we obtain GAALD and GAALD w.r.t. ​
\mathfrak{S}
for high jets and the combination of closed subschemes.

Theorem 0.15

(= Theorem
9.1
, GAALD)
.

Let
(X,V)
be a directed projective manifold, and let
Z_{1},\dots,Z_{q}
be closed subschemes of
X
. Set

\ell_{k}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\max\{|I|\mid I\subset J,\,\bigcap_{i\in I}Z_{i}^{(k)}\neq\varnothing\}.

For each
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, let
f\colon(\mathbb{C},T_{\mathbb{C}})\to(X,V)
be a non-constant holomorphic curve such that
f(\mathbb{C})\not\subset\Supp(Z_{1}+\dots+Z_{q})
.
Then,

\sum_{i=1}^{q}m_{f}(r,Z_{i})+T_{f_{[k]}}(r,\mathcal{O}_{X_{k}}(\bm{\ell}))+\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\ell_{j}\mathcal{N}([Z_{f_{[j]}^{\prime}}],r)\leqslant m_{f_{[k]}}(r,\bigvee_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\subset J\\
|I|=\ell_{k}\end{subarray}}\sum_{i\in I}Z_{i}^{(k)})+S_{f}(r),

where
\bm{\ell}=(\ell_{0},\dots,\ell_{k-1})\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{k}
.

Theorem 0.16

(= Theorem
9.6
, GAALD w.r.t. ​
\mathfrak{S}
)
.

Let
(X,V)
be a directed projective manifold, and let
Z_{1},\dots,Z_{q}
be closed subschemes of
X
.
Choose a linear system
\mathfrak{S}
on
X
satisfying that
\mathcal{I}_{Z_{i}}=\mathfrak{s}_{\supset Z_{i}}
for each
1\leqslant i\leqslant q
. Set

\ell_{k,\mathfrak{S}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\max\{|I|\mid I\subset J,\,\bigcap_{i\in I}(Z_{i})_{\mathfrak{S}}^{[k]}\supsetneqq Z_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}(X_{k},\mathfrak{S})}\}.

Let
k_{\mathfrak{S}}
be the minimum of
k
such that
\ell_{k,\mathfrak{S}}=0
.
Let
f\colon(\mathbb{C},T_{\mathbb{C}})\to(X,V)
be a non-constant holomorphic curve such that
f_{[k_{\mathfrak{S}}]}(\mathbb{C})\not\subset\Bs(\mathfrak{W}(X_{k_{\mathfrak{S}}},\mathfrak{S}))
.
Suppose that
f(\mathbb{C})\not\subset\Supp(Z_{1}+\dots+Z_{q})
.
Then,

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{q}m_{f}(r,Z_{i})+T_{f_{[k_{\mathfrak{S}}]}}(r,\mathcal{O}_{X_{k_{\mathfrak{S}}}}(\bm{\ell}_{\mathfrak{S}}))+\sum_{j=0}^{k_{\mathfrak{S}}-1}\ell_{j,\mathfrak{S}}\mathcal{N}([Z_{f_{[j]}^{\prime}}],r)

\displaystyle\leqslant\,

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{k_{\mathfrak{S}}}(\ell_{j-1,\mathfrak{S}}-2\ell_{j,\mathfrak{S}}+\ell_{j+1,\mathfrak{S}})m_{f_{[j]}}(r,Z_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}(X_{j},\mathfrak{S})})+S_{f}(r),

where
\bm{\ell}_{\mathfrak{S}}=(\ell_{0,\mathfrak{S}},\dots,\ell_{k_{\mathfrak{S}}-1,\mathfrak{S}})\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{k_{\mathfrak{S}}}
and we make the conventions that
\ell_{-1,\mathfrak{S}}=q
, and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}(X_{0},\mathfrak{S})=\mathfrak{s}
.

1.
Directed varieties and Blow-up

1.1.
Directed varieties

Let us consider a pair
(X,V)
consisting of a complex manifold
X
of dimension
n
equipped with a linear subspace
V\subset T_{X}
.
More precisely, if
X
is connected, the total space of
V
is by definition an irreducible closed analytic subspace of the total space of
T_{X}
such that each fiber
V_{x}=V\cap T_{X,x}
is a vector subspace of
T_{X,x}
.
If
X
is not connected,
V
should be irreducible merely over each connected component of
X
, but we will typically assume that the varieties are connected.

Let
\mathcal{W}\subset\mathcal{T}_{X}^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}
be the sheaf of
1
-forms vanishing on
V
.
Notice that
\mathcal{W}
is indeed the direct image sheaf
(\pi_{1})_{\ast}\mathcal{O}_{\textnormal{P}(V)}(1)
, where
\pi_{1}\colon\textnormal{P}(V)\subset\textnormal{P}(T_{X})\hookrightarrow X
is a proper morphism.
It follows from the direct image theorem (cf.
[
Gra60
]
) that
\mathcal{W}
is coherent.
Take a small open subset
U\subset X
, and choose some local sections
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{q}\in\mathcal{W}(U)
generating
\mathcal{W}
at every point of
U
.
Then
V
is locally defined by

V_{x}=\{\xi\in T_{X,x}\mid\alpha_{j}(x)\cdot\xi=0,\,\forall 1\leqslant j\leqslant q\},\vskip-2.15277pt

for every point
x\in U
.
We can associate to
V
a coherent sheaf
\mathcal{V}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\mathcal{W}^{\perp}=\mathcal{H}om_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}(\mathcal{T}_{X}^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}/\mathcal{W},\mathcal{O}_{X})\subset\mathcal{T}_{X}
,
which is a saturated subsheaf of
\mathcal{T}_{X}
, i.e., such that
\mathcal{T}_{X}/\mathcal{V}
has no torsion.
Consequently,
\mathcal{V}
is also reflexive, namely,
\mathcal{V}^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}=\mathcal{V}
.
We refer to such a pair as a (complex) directed variety.
In general, we will think of
V
as a linear space, rather than considering the associated saturated subsheaf
\mathcal{V}\subset\mathcal{T}_{X}
.
A morphism
\varphi\colon(X,V)\to(Y,W)
in the category of complex directed varieties is a holomorphic map
\varphi\colon X\to Y
such that
\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\varphi(V)\subset W
, where
\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\varphi
is the differential of
\varphi
.

The rank at each point
x\in X
is given by a function
x\mapsto\dim_{\mathbb{C}}V_{x}
, which is Zariski lower semi-continuous and may a priori jump.
The rank
r\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\rank V
of
V
is defined as the dimension of
V_{x}
at a generic point.
The dimension may be larger at non-generic points; this happens e.g., on
X=\mathbb{C}^{n}
for the rank
1
linear space
V
generated by the Euler vector field:
V_{z}=\mathbb{C}\sum_{1\leqslant j\leq n}z_{j}\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{j}}
for
z\neq 0
, and
V_{0}=\mathbb{C}^{n}
.

We believe that directed varieties are useful even when studying the "absolute case" (where
V=T_{X}
).
By (
2.6
), any natural embedding
(X,V)\hookrightarrow(X,T_{X})
induces a certain functorial constructions in the category of directed varieties.
We think of directed varieties as a kind of “relative situation”, covering, e.g., the case when
V
is the relative tangent space to a holomorphic map
X\to S
.
It is important to notice that the local sections of
\mathcal{V}
need not generate the fibers of
V
at singular points, as one sees already in the case of the Euler vector field when
n\geq 2
.
We also want to emphasize that no assumption need be made about the Lie bracket tensor
[\bullet,\bullet]\colon\mathcal{V}\times\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{T}_{X}/\mathcal{V}
,
which amounts to saying that we do not assume any kind of integrability for
\mathcal{V}
or
\mathcal{W}
.

The singular set
\Sing(V)
is defined by the set of points where
\mathcal{V}
is not locally free.
It can also be characterized as the indeterminacy set of the (meromorphic) classifying map to the Grassmannian of
r
-dimensional subspaces of
T_{X}
as follows:

(1.1)

\displaystyle\alpha\colon X

\displaystyle\dashrightarrow\Gr(r,T_{X})

\displaystyle z

\displaystyle\longmapsto V_{z}.

We thus have
V_{\restriction X\setminus\Sing(V)}=\alpha^{\ast}S
, where
S\to\Gr(r,T_{X})
is the tautological subbundle of
\Gr(r,T_{X})
.
The singular set
\Sing(V)
is an analytic subset of
X
of codimension at least
2
, and hence
V
is always a holomorphic subbundle outside an analytic subset of codimension at least
2
.
Thanks to this remark, one can most often treat linear spaces as vector bundles (possibly modulo passing to the Zariski closure along
\Sing(V)
).

In addition, we call a (complex) directed variety
(X,V)
a (complex) directed manifold, if
V
is a holomorphic subbundle of
T_{X}
.
And we say that a directed manifold
(X,V)
is a directed projective manifold, in the case where
X
is a complex projective manifold.

1.2.
Blow-up of directed varieties

For a complex variety
X
and an ideal sheaf
\mathcal{I}\subset\mathcal{O}_{X}
, we have the blow-up of
X
with respect to
\mathcal{I}

\upsilon\colon\widehat{X}=\Bl_{\mathcal{I}}(X)\longrightarrow X,

where
\Bl_{\mathcal{I}}(X)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\Proj\bigoplus_{d\geqslant 0}\mathcal{I}^{d}
(see Chapter II, Section 7 of Hartshorne’s book
[
Har77
]
for more details).
If
Z_{\mathcal{I}}
is the closed subscheme of
X
with the ideal sheaf
\mathcal{I}
, then we also call
\upsilon\colon\widehat{X}\to X
the blow-up of
X
with center
Z_{\mathcal{I}}
.
Moreover, we see that
\upsilon^{-1}\mathcal{I}\cdot\mathcal{O}_{\widehat{X}}
associates to an effective Cartier divisor
E
on
\widehat{X}
, called the exceptional divisor of
\upsilon
.
It amounts to saying that

\mathcal{O}_{\widehat{X}}(-E)=\upsilon^{-1}\mathcal{I}\cdot\mathcal{O}_{\widehat{X}}.

Apparently,
\upsilon_{\restriction U}\colon U\to X
is an open embedding, where
U=\widehat{X}\setminus E
.

Now, we are going to generalize the blow-ups to complex directed varieties.
Let
(X,V)
be a complex directed variety with
V
being possibly singular, and let
\upsilon
be the blow-up of
X
with respect to
\mathcal{I}
as above. We thus get a directed variety
(\widehat{X},\widehat{V})
by taking
\widehat{V}
to be the Zariski closure of
(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\upsilon)^{-1}(V^{\prime})
,
where
V^{\prime}=V_{\restriction X^{\prime}}
is the restriction of
V
over a Zariski open subset
X^{\prime}\subset X\setminus(\Supp(\mathcal{O}_{X}/\mathcal{I})\cup\Sing(V))
.
This construction is independent of the choice of
X^{\prime}
, thanks to the Zariski density of
X^{\prime}
.
Therefore, we say the morphism of directed varieties

\upsilon\colon(\widehat{X},\widehat{V})\longrightarrow(X,V)

is the blow-up of
(X,V)
with respect to
\mathcal{I}
.

Let
f\colon\bigtriangleup(R)\to X
be a (holomorphic) tangent curve of the directed variety
(X,V)
, in other words,
f^{\prime}(z)\in V_{f(z)}
for every
z\in\bigtriangleup(R)
,
where
\bigtriangleup(R)
is the disk of center
0
and radius
R
in the complex plane
\mathbb{C}
.
Suppose that
f
is non-constant and
f(\bigtriangleup(R))\not\subset\Supp(\mathcal{O}_{X}/\mathcal{I})\cup\Sing(V)
.
Obviously, there exists a unique curve
\hat{f}\colon\bigtriangleup(R)\to\widehat{X}
such that its graph is the same to the (Euclidean) closure of the graph of
\upsilon_{\restriction U}^{-1}(f)
, where
U=\widehat{X}\setminus E
.
Observe that the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta(R)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

commutes, and
\hat{f}
is a non-constant tangent curve of
(\widehat{X},\widehat{V})
.
We thus call
\hat{f}
the lifting of
f
to
(\widehat{X},\widehat{V})
.

Moreover, we claim that
\Sing(\widehat{V})\subset\upsilon^{-1}(\Sing(V))
.
Actually, the composition
\alpha\circ\upsilon
gives a rational map
\widehat{\alpha}\colon\widehat{X}\dashrightarrow\Gr(r,T_{X})
, where
\alpha\colon X\dashrightarrow\Gr(r,T_{X})
is defined by (
1.1
).
We easily check that
\widehat{\alpha}
coincides with the meromorphic extension of
\alpha\circ\upsilon_{\restriction U^{\prime}}
.
Hence
\Sing(\widehat{V})=\Ind(\widehat{\alpha})\subset\upsilon^{-1}(\Ind(\alpha))=\upsilon^{-1}(\Sing(V))
.

Remark 1.1
.

In Section 2.C of
[
Dem20
]
, Demailly also introduced the blow-up in the category of directed varieties.
Notice that
\upsilon
restricted on
\upsilon^{-1}(X^{\prime})
is a (holomorphic) isomorphism, namely, biholomorphism for any Zariski open subset
X^{\prime}\subset X\setminus(\Supp(\mathcal{O}_{X}/\mathcal{I})\cup\Sing(V))
.
Therefore, our construction of the blow-up is identified with the construction in
[
Dem20
]
.

2.
Jets and jet differentials

2.1.
Jet spaces

Let us first recall the classic jet space.

Let
X
be a complex manifold of dimension
n
.
Given a point
x\in X
, a germ of holomorphic curves
f\colon(\mathbb{C},0)\to(X,x)
is an equivalence class of pairs
(U,f_{U})
,
where
U\subset\mathbb{C}
is an open neighborhood of the origin
0
,
f_{U}
is a holomorphic curve from
U
to
X
such that
f_{U}(0)=x
, and where
(U_{1},f_{U_{1}})
and
(U_{2},f_{U_{2}})
are equivalent if
f_{U_{1}}
and
f_{U_{2}}
agree on
U_{1}\cap U_{2}
.
Moreover, for the germs of holomorphic curves
f,g\colon(\mathbb{C},0)\to(X,x)
,
one defines the equivalence relation
f\overset{k}{\sim}g
if and only if
f^{(\ell)}(0)=g^{(\ell)}(0)
for all
0\leqslant\ell\leqslant k
,
where the derivatives are computed in some (equivalently, any) local coordinate system near
x
.
The equivalence class of
f
is denoted by
j_{k}f
.
Set

\displaystyle J_{k}X_{x}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\{j_{k}f\mid f:(\mathbb{C},0)\to(X,x)\},

\displaystyle J_{k}X

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\bigcup_{x\in X}J_{k}X_{x}.

One immediately gets a natural projection

p_{k}\colon J_{k}X\longrightarrow X

defined by
p_{k}(j_{k}f)=f(0)
.
Apparently,
J_{k}X
is a complex manifold, and
p_{k}
gives a
\mathbb{C}^{nk}
-fiber bundle over
X
, which is called
k
-jet bundle over
X
.
In the case when
k=1
,
J_{1}X=T_{X}
is holomorphic tangent bundle over
X
.
However, for
k\geqslant 2
, there is no such linear structure in
k
-jet bundle.

Furthermore, following Definition 5.1 in
[
Dem97
]
, one has the jet spaces over a complex directed manifold
(X,V)
.
Let
(X,V)
be a complex directed manifold such that
V
is a holomorphic subbundle of
T_{X}
.
One denotes by
J_{k}V
the
k
-jet bundle of
X
tangent to
V
, consisting of all
k
-jets
j_{k}f
such that
f^{\prime}(z)\in V_{f(z)}
for all
z
in some neighborhood of
0
in the complex plane.
Apparently,
J_{k}V
is a subbundle of
J_{k}X
. For shortness, one hereinafter writes

p_{k,V}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=p_{k\restriction J_{k}V}\colon J_{k}V\longrightarrow X.

The following construction indicates that
J_{k}V
is indeed a
\mathbb{C}^{rk}
-fiber bundle over
X
under this projection
p_{k,V}
, where
r=\rank V
.

Given any open subset
U\subset X
, for each
0\leqslant\ell\leqslant k-1
, one can define a
\mathbb{C}
-linear map

(2.1)

\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}_{U,V}^{[\ell]}\colon\mathcal{V}^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}(U)\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}(p_{k,V}^{-1}(U))

by
\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}_{U,V}^{[\ell]}\omega(j_{k}f)=A^{(\ell)}(0)
for every holomorphic 1-form
\omega\in\mathcal{V}^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}(U)
and
j_{k}f\in p_{k,V}^{-1}(U)\subset J_{k}V
,
where
A
is a germ of functions on
(\mathbb{C},0)
such that
f^{\ast}\omega=A(z)\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}z
.
Obviously, for any
j_{k}f\in J_{k}V
, one has
\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}_{U,V}^{[\ell]}(\res_{V}\omega)(j_{k}f)=\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}_{U,T_{X}}^{[\ell]}\omega(j_{k}f)
,
where
\res_{V}\colon\mathcal{T}_{X}^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}\to\mathcal{V}^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}
is a quotient morphism induced by the restriction morphism
\mathcal{O}(T_{X})\to\mathcal{O}(V)
.
Take an open subset
U\subset X
such that there exist
r
local sections
\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{r}\in\mathcal{V}^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}(U)
generating
V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}
at every point of
U
.
Thus one obtains a local trivialization of
p_{k,V}^{-1}(U)
given by

(2.2)

\displaystyle p_{k,V}\times(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}_{U,V}^{[\ell]}\omega_{i})_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leqslant i\leqslant r\hskip 8.19447pt\\
0\leqslant\ell\leqslant k-1\end{subarray}}\colon p_{k,V}^{-1}(U)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow U\times\mathbb{C}^{rk}

\displaystyle j_{k}f

\displaystyle\longmapsto\left(f(0),(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}_{U,V}^{[\ell]}\omega_{i}(j_{k}f))_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leqslant i\leqslant r\hskip 8.19447pt\\
0\leqslant\ell\leqslant k-1\end{subarray}}\right).

In the particular case where
\omega=\res_{V}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\sigma
for some
\sigma\in\mathcal{O}(U)
, slightly extending the notation, one writes

(2.3)

\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}_{U,V}^{[\ell]}\sigma\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\begin{cases}p_{k,V}^{\ast}\sigma&\ell=0,\\[5.0pt]
\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}_{U,V}^{[\ell-1]}\omega&1\leqslant\ell\leqslant k.\end{cases}

It is evident that
\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}_{U,V}^{[\ell]}\sigma(j_{k}f)=(\sigma\circ f)^{(\ell)}(0)
for every
\sigma\in\mathcal{O}(U)
and
j_{k}f\in p_{k,V}^{-1}(U)\subset J_{k}V
.
When no confusion can arise, one simply writes
\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}^{[\ell]}
for brevity.

2.2.
Demailly-Semple jet towers

Let us recall the Demailly-Semple jet tower (see Section 4 and 5 of
[
Dem97
]
for more details).
Let us start from
(X_{0},V_{0})=(X,V)
, for a given directed manifold
(X,V)
.
In the spirit of
[
Sem54
]
, Demailly
[
Dem97
]
inductively constructed a sequence of directed manifolds
(X_{\bullet},V_{\bullet})
as follows.

Let
X_{k}=\textnormal{P}(V_{k-1})
with its natural projection
\pi_{k}
to
X_{k-1}
, for each
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
.
For every point
(x,[v])\in X_{k}=\textnormal{P}(V_{k-1})
associated with a tangent vector
v\in(V_{k-1})_{x}\setminus\{0\}
for
x\in X_{k-1}
, one defines the tangent subbundle
V_{k}\subset T_{X_{k}}
by

(V_{k})_{(x,[v])}=\{\xi\in(T_{X_{k}})_{(x,[v])}\mid\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\pi_{k}(\xi)\in\mathbb{C}\,v\},\quad\mathbb{C}\,v\subset(V_{k-1})_{x},

where
\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\pi_{k}\colon T_{X_{k}}\to\pi_{k}^{\ast}T_{X_{k-1}}
is the differential of
\pi_{k}
.
One refers to the directed manifold
(X_{k},V_{k})
as the Demailly-Semple
k
-jet tower of
(X,V)
.

Remark 2.1
.

Technically, the projective manifold
\textnormal{P}(V)
of lines of the vector bundle
V
is identified with the projective bundle
\mathbb{P}(V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}})\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\Proj\bigoplus_{d\geqslant 0}\Sym^{d}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}
.

On
X_{k}=\textnormal{P}(V_{k-1})
, one has the tautological line bundle
\mathcal{O}_{X_{k}}(-1)\subset\pi_{k}^{\ast}V_{k-1}
such that
\mathcal{O}_{X_{k}}(-1)_{(x,[v])}=\mathbb{C}\,v
.
Actually, the definition of
V_{k}
gives a exact sequence

(2.4)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{X_{k}/X_{k-1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}_{X_{k}}(-1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\pi_{k})_{\ast}}

where
T_{X_{k}/X_{k-1}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\ker(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\pi_{k})
denotes the relative tangent bundle of
\pi_{k}
.
Moreover, thinking of
X_{k}
as a projective bundle
X_{k}=\mathbb{P}(V_{k-1}^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}})
, one gets the Euler exact sequence

(2.5)

0\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{X_{k}}\longrightarrow\pi_{k}^{\ast}V_{k-1}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{X_{k}}(1)\longrightarrow T_{X_{k}/X_{k-1}}\longrightarrow 0.

It is evident that

\dim X_{k}=n+k(r-1),\qquad\rank V_{k}=r,

where
r\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\rank V
.
Hence, taking the determinants of (
2.5
), one obtains

\displaystyle\det T_{X_{k}/X_{k-1}}

\displaystyle=\pi_{k}^{\ast}\det V_{k-1}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{X_{k}}(r),

\displaystyle\det V_{k}

\displaystyle=\pi_{k}^{\ast}\det V_{k-1}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{X_{k}}(r-1).

Obviously,
\pi_{k}
induces a morphism of directed manifolds

\pi_{k}\colon(X_{k},V_{k})\longrightarrow(X_{k-1},V_{k-1}),

for each
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
.
One sees that this construction is functorial.
That is to say, for every morphism of directed manifolds
\varphi\colon(X,V)\to(Y,W)
, there is a commutative diagram

(2.6)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X_{1},V_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X,V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Y_{1},W_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Y,W),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}

where
\varphi_{1}
is induced by the differential
\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\varphi
restricted on
V
.
Notice that
\varphi_{1}
is holomorphic if
\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\varphi_{\restriction V}\colon V\to\varphi^{\ast}W
is injective.

Observe that
\mathcal{O}_{X_{k}}(-1)\hookrightarrow\pi_{k}^{\ast}V_{k-1}
is a canonical injection on
X_{k}=\mathbb{P}(V_{k-1}^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}})
,
and the composition with
(\pi_{k-1})_{\ast}
(analogue for order
k-1
of the arrow
(\pi_{k})_{\ast}
in sequence (
2.4
)) gives a line bundle morphism over
X_{k}

(2.7)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}_{X_{k}}(-1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{k}^{\ast}V_{k-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{k}^{\ast}\mathcal{O}_{X_{k-1}}(-1),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{k}^{\ast}(\pi_{k-1})_{\ast}}

for any integer
k\geqslant 2
, which admits
\varGamma_{k}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\textnormal{P}(T_{X_{k-1}/X_{k-2}})\subset X_{k}
as its zero divisor.
Immediately, one infers that

(2.8)

\mathcal{O}_{X_{k}}(1)=\pi_{k}^{\ast}\mathcal{O}_{X_{k-1}}(1)\otimes\mathcal{O}(\varGamma_{k}).

Now, for any integer
0\leqslant j\leqslant k-1
, one defines the composition of projections

(2.9)

\pi_{j,k}=\pi_{j+1}\circ\pi_{j+2}\circ\dots\circ\pi_{k}\colon X_{k}\longrightarrow X_{j}.

For the sake of convenience, one writes

(2.10)

\mathcal{O}_{X_{k}}(\bm{a})\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\pi_{1,k}^{\ast}\mathcal{O}_{X_{1}}(a_{1})\otimes\pi_{2,k}^{\ast}\mathcal{O}_{X_{2}}(a_{2})\otimes\dots\otimes\mathcal{O}_{X_{k}}(a_{k}),

for every
\bm{a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k})\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}
.
It follows from (
2.8
) that

\pi_{j,k}^{\ast}\mathcal{O}_{X_{j}}(1)=\mathcal{O}_{X_{k}}(1)\otimes\mathcal{O}_{X_{k}}(-\pi_{j+1,k}^{\ast}\varGamma_{j+1}-\dots-\varGamma_{k}).

One thus gets

(2.11)

\mathcal{O}_{X_{k}}(\bm{a})=\mathcal{O}_{X_{k}}(b_{k})\otimes\mathcal{O}_{X_{k}}(-\bm{b}\cdot\varGamma),

where

\displaystyle\bm{b}

\displaystyle=(b_{1},\dots,b_{k})\in\mathbb{Z}^{k},\quad b_{j}=a_{1}+\dots+a_{j},

\displaystyle\bm{b}

\displaystyle\cdot\varGamma=\sum_{1\leqslant j\leqslant k-1}b_{j}\pi_{j+1,k}^{\ast}\varGamma_{j+1}.

2.3.
Lifting of curves to Demailly-Semple jet towers

Let
f\colon\bigtriangleup(R)\to X
be a (holomorphic) tangent curve of the directed manifold
(X,V)
, i.e.,
f^{\prime}(z)\in V_{f(z)}
for every
z\in\bigtriangleup(R)
.
Suppose that
f
is non-constant. Then we can write
f^{\prime}(z)=(z-z_{0})^{m}u(z)
with
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
u(z_{0})\neq 0
, for every
z_{0}\in\bigtriangleup(R)
. Here
m>0
only when
z_{0}
is a stationary point. Obviously,
[u(z_{0})]
gives a well-defined and unique tangent line in
\textnormal{P}(V_{f(z_{0})})
, even at stationary points, and the map

\displaystyle f_{[1]}\colon\bigtriangleup(R)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow X_{1}

\displaystyle z

\displaystyle\longmapsto f_{[1]}(z)=(f(z),[u(z)])

is holomorphic.
By definition
f^{\prime}(z)\in\mathcal{O}_{X_{1}}(-1)_{f_{[1]}(z)}=\mathbb{C}\,u(z)
, hence the derivative
f^{\prime}
defines a section

f^{\prime}\colon T_{\bigtriangleup(R)}\longrightarrow f_{[1]}^{\ast}\mathcal{O}_{X_{1}}(-1).

Moreover, seeing that

\pi_{1}\circ f_{[1]}(z)=f(z),\quad(\pi_{1})_{\ast}f_{[1]}^{\prime}(z)=f^{\prime}(z)\in\mathbb{C}\,u(z),

we have
f_{[1]}^{\prime}(z)\in(V_{1})_{(f(z),[u(z)])}=(V_{1})_{f_{[1]}(z)}
, which implies that
f_{[1]}
is a non-constant tangent curve of
(X_{1},V_{1})
. We thus call
f_{[1]}
the canonical lifting of
f
to
(X_{1},V_{1})
.

Furthermore, every non-constant tangent curve
f
of
(X,V)
inductively lifts to a unique non-constant tangent curve
f_{[k]}
of
(X_{k},V_{k})
, for each
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
. And the derivative
f_{[k-1]}^{\prime}
gives rise to a section

(2.12)

f_{[k-1]}^{\prime}\colon T_{\bigtriangleup(R)}\longrightarrow f_{[k]}^{\ast}\mathcal{O}_{X_{k}}(-1).

Now, let us investigate the multiplicity of tangent curves and their liftings. We define the multiplicity
m(f,z_{0})
of a curves
f\colon\bigtriangleup(R)\to X
at a point
z_{0}
to be the smallest positive integer
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
such that
f^{(m)}(z_{0})\neq 0
. That is, we can write
f^{\prime}(z)=(z-z_{0})^{m-1}u(z)
such that
u(z_{0})\neq 0
. One easily sees that
m(f_{[k]},z)
is non-increasing with
k
. What’s more, Demailly verified the following proposition.

Proposition 2.2

(Proposition 5.11,
[
Dem97
]
)
.

Let
f\colon(\mathbb{C},0)\to(X,x)
be a germ of non-constant curves tangent to
V
. Then we have
m(f_{[j-2]},0)\geqslant m(f_{[j-1]},0)
for any integer
j\geqslant 2
, and the inequality is strict if and only if
f_{[j]}(0)\in\varGamma_{j}
.
Conversely, if
x_{k}\in X_{k}
is an arbitrary point and
m_{0},m_{1},\dots,m_{k-1}
is a non-increasing sequence of positive integers satisfying

m_{j-2}>m_{j-1}\quad\text{if and only if }\pi_{j,k}(x_{k})\in\varGamma_{j},

for all integer
2\leqslant j\leqslant k
, then there exist a germ of curves
f\colon(\mathbb{C},0)\to(X,x)
tangent to
V
such that
f_{[k]}(0)=x_{k}
and
m(f_{[j]},0)=m_{j}
for all integer
0\leqslant j\leqslant k-1
, where
x=\pi_{0,k}(x_{k})
.

Observe that we can always take
m_{k-1}=1
. Thus we have

Corollary 2.3

(Corollary 5.12,
[
Dem97
]
)
.

For any
x_{k}\in X_{k}
, there is a germ of curves
f\colon(\mathbb{C},0)\to(X,x)
such that
f_{[k]}(0)=x_{k}
and
f^{\prime}_{[k-1]}(0)\neq 0
. Moreover, if
x_{k}
is taken in a sufficiently small neighborhood, then the germ
f=f_{x_{k}}
can be taken to depend holomorphically on
x_{k}
.

Therefore,
f\colon(\mathbb{C},0)\to(X,x)
is regular at the origin (i.e.,
f^{\prime}(0)\neq 0
) if and only if
x_{k}=f_{[k]}(0)\in X_{k}
satisfies
\pi_{j,k}(x_{k})\not\in\varGamma_{j}
for all
2\leqslant j\leqslant k
. Hence, we naturally define

\displaystyle X_{k}^{\reg}

\displaystyle=\bigcap_{2\leqslant j\leqslant k}\pi_{j,k}^{-1}(X_{j}\setminus\varGamma_{j}),

\displaystyle X_{k}^{\sing}

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{2\leqslant j\leqslant k}\pi_{j,k}^{-1}(\varGamma_{j})=X_{k}\setminus X_{k}^{\reg}.

2.4.
Green-Griffiths jet differentials

We now introduce the concept of jet differentials in the sense of Green-Griffiths
[
GG80
]
.

Let
\mathbb{G}_{k}
be the group of germs of
k
-jets of biholomorphisms of
(\mathbb{C},0)
, i.e., the group of germs of biholomorphic maps

\displaystyle\varphi\colon(\mathbb{C},0)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow(\mathbb{C},0)

\displaystyle z

\displaystyle\longmapsto\varphi(z)=a_{1}z+a_{2}z^{2}+\dots+a_{k}z^{k},

where
a_{1}\in\mathbb{C}^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\times$}}
,
a_{j}\in\mathbb{C}
,
j\geqslant 2
, and the composition law is taken modulo
z^{k+1}
.
Apparently,
\mathbb{G}_{k}
gives a natural reparametrization action on
J_{k}V
,
\varphi\cdot j_{k}f\mapsto j_{k}(f\circ\varphi)
.
Observe that the commutator subgroup
\mathbb{G}_{k}^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=[\mathbb{G}_{k},\mathbb{G}_{k}]
is the group of
k
-jets of biholomorphisms tangent to the identity. Thus there is an exact sequence of groups

1\longrightarrow\mathbb{G}_{k}^{\prime}\longrightarrow\mathbb{G}_{k}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\times$}}\longrightarrow 1,

where
\mathbb{G}_{k}\to\mathbb{C}^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\times$}}
is the morphism
\varphi\mapsto\varphi^{\prime}(0)
, and
\mathbb{C}^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\times$}}
is interpreted as the group of homotheties
\varphi(z)=\lambda z
.
Suppose
j_{k}f
is parametrized by
(f^{\prime},f^{\prime\prime},\ldots,f^{(k)})
.
Thereupon a reparametrization action
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\times$}}
on
J_{k}V
is described by

\lambda\cdot(f^{\prime},f^{\prime\prime},\ldots,f^{(k)})=(\lambda f^{\prime},\lambda^{2}f^{\prime\prime},\ldots,\lambda^{k}f^{(k)}).

Following
[
GG80
]
, we consider homogeneous polynomials
Q
on
J_{k}V
of weighted degree
m
with respect to the reparametrization action of
\mathbb{C}^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\times$}}
, which amounts to satisfying that

Q(\lambda f^{\prime},\lambda^{2}f^{\prime\prime},\ldots,\lambda^{k}f^{(k)})=\lambda^{m}Q(f^{\prime},f^{\prime\prime},\ldots,f^{(k)})

for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\times$}}
and
(f^{\prime},f^{\prime\prime},\ldots,f^{(k)})\in J_{k}V
.
Notice that the concept of polynomial on the fibers of
J_{k}V
makes sense, for any coordinate changes
z\mapsto w=\Psi(z)
on
X
induce a polynomial transition automorphism on the fibers of
J_{k}V
, given by

(2.13)

(\Psi\circ f)^{(\ell)}=\Psi^{\prime}(f)\cdot f^{(\ell)}+\sum_{s=2}^{\ell}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{j}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{s}\\
\|\bm{j}\|=\ell\end{subarray}}c_{\bm{j}}\Psi^{(s)}(f)\cdot(f^{(j_{1})}\cdots f^{(j_{s})})

with suitable integer constants
c_{\bm{j}}
(these are easily computed by induction on
s
), where
\|\bm{j}\|\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=j_{1}+\dots+j_{s}
.

Green-Griffiths jet differentials sheaf
\mathcal{E}_{k,m}^{GG}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}
of order
k
and weighted degree
m
, is a locally free sheaf consisting of these polynomials
Q
, i.e., for any open subset
U\subset X
,

\mathcal{E}_{k,m}^{GG}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}(U)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\{Q\in\mathcal{O}(p_{k,V}^{-1}(U))\mid Q(\lambda\cdot j_{k}f)=\lambda^{m}Q(j_{k}f),\forall j_{k}f\in p_{k,V}^{-1}(U),\forall\lambda\in\mathbb{C}^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\times$}}\}.

The corresponding vector bundle is denoted by
E_{k,m}^{GG}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}
, and hereby called Green-Griffiths jet differentials bundle of order
k
and weighted degree
m
.

For a holomorphic subbundle
V\subset T_{X}
, there is a natural inclusion
J_{k}V\hookrightarrow J_{k}X
. Hence, the restriction induces a surjective morphism
\mathcal{E}_{k,m}^{GG}T_{X}^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{E}_{k,m}^{GG}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}
.
This implies that
\mathcal{E}_{k,m}^{GG}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}
is a quotient of
\mathcal{E}_{k,m}^{GG}T_{X}^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}
.

For any Green-Griffiths jet differentials
Q\in\mathcal{E}_{k,m}^{GG}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}
, it can be decomposed into multihomogeneous components of multidegree
\bm{\ell}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\dots,\ell_{k})\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
in
f^{\prime},f^{\prime\prime},\dots,\allowbreak f^{(k)}
(the decomposition is certainly dependent on the choice of coordinate).
It is evident that the coordinate change must keep the weighted degree constant, that is,
|\bm{\ell}|_{k}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\ell_{1}+2\ell_{2}+\dots+k\ell_{k}=m
.
Moreover, investigate the partial weighted degree
|\bm{\ell}|_{s}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\ell_{1}+2\ell_{2}+\dots+s\ell_{s}
of order
s
.
For
1\leqslant s\leqslant k
, it follows from (
2.13
) that a coordinate change
f\mapsto\Psi\circ f
transforms every monomial
(f^{(\bullet)})^{\bm{\ell}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=(f^{\prime})^{\ell_{1}}(f^{\prime\prime})^{\ell_{2}}\cdots(f^{(k)})^{\ell_{k}}
into a polynomial
((\Psi\circ f)^{(\bullet)})^{\bm{\ell}}
whose non-zero monomials have a larger or equal partial weighted degree of order
s
.
In particular, if
\ell_{s+1}=\dots=\ell_{k}=0
, it keeps the partial weighted degree of order
s
the same.

Therefore, for each integer
1\leqslant s\leqslant k-1
, one gets a well-defined decreasing filtration
F_{s}^{\bullet}
on
\mathcal{E}_{k,m}^{GG}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}
as follows.
For every positive integer
p\leqslant m
, the sheaf
F_{s}^{p}(\mathcal{E}_{k,m}^{GG}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}})
is the set of polynomials
Q(f^{\prime},f^{\prime\prime},\dots,f^{(k)})\in\mathcal{E}_{k,m}^{GG}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}
involving only monomials
(f^{(\bullet)})^{\bm{\ell}}
with
|\ell|_{s}\geqslant p
, regardless of the choice of coordinate.

One denotes the graded terms associated with the filtration
F_{k-1}^{p}(\mathcal{E}_{k,m}^{GG}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}})
by

\Gr_{k-1}^{p}(\mathcal{E}_{k,m}^{GG}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}})=F_{k-1}^{p}(\mathcal{E}_{k,m}^{GG}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}})/F_{k-1}^{p+1}(\mathcal{E}_{k,m}^{GG}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}).

Technically,
\Gr_{k-1}^{p}(\mathcal{E}_{k,m}^{GG}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}})
are precisely the homogeneous polynomials
Q
whose monomials
(f^{\bullet})^{\bm{\ell}}
all have partial weighted degree
|\ell|_{k-1}=p
.
Obviously, the degree
\ell_{k}
in
f^{(k)}
of each monomial needs to satisfy
m-p=k\ell_{k}
, and
\Gr_{k-1}^{p}(\mathcal{E}_{k,m}^{GG}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}})=0
unless
k|m-p
.
The transition automorphisms of the graded bundle are induced by coordinate changes
f\mapsto\Psi\circ f
, and they are described by substituting the arguments of
Q(f^{\prime},f^{\prime\prime},\dots,f^{(k)})
according to formula (
2.13
), namely,

f^{(j)}\mapsto(\Psi\circ f)^{(j)}
for
j<k
, and
f^{(k)}\mapsto\Psi^{\prime}(f)\circ f^{(k)}
for
j=k
(when
j=k
, the other terms fall in the next stage
F^{p+1}_{k-1}
of the filtration).
Hence
f^{(k)}
behaves as an element of
V\subset T_{X}
under coordinate changes. One infers

\Gr_{k-1}^{m-k\ell_{k}}(\mathcal{E}_{k,m}^{GG}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}})\cong\mathcal{E}_{k-1,m-k\ell_{k}}^{GG}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}\otimes\Sym^{\ell_{k}}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}.

Combining all filtrations
F_{s}^{\bullet}
together, one inductively finds a filtration
F^{\bullet}
on
\mathcal{E}_{k,m}^{GG}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}
such that the graded terms are

(2.14)

\Gr^{\bm{\ell}}(\mathcal{E}_{k,m}^{GG}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}})\cong\Sym^{\ell_{1}}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}\otimes\Sym^{\ell_{2}}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}\otimes\dots\otimes\Sym^{\ell_{k}}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}},

for
\bm{\ell}\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
with
|\bm{\ell}|_{k}=m
.

Associated with the graded algebra sheaf
\mathcal{E}_{k,\bullet}^{GG}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\bigoplus_{m\geqslant 0}\mathcal{E}_{k,m}^{GG}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}
, one has an analytic fiber bundle

(2.15)

X_{k}^{GG}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\Proj\mathcal{E}_{k,\bullet}^{GG}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}=(J_{k}V\setminus(X\times\{0\}))/\mathbb{C}^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\times$}}

over
X
, where
J_{k}V\setminus(X\times\{0\})
is the set of non-constant
k
-jets. By construction, one easily sees that
X_{k}^{GG}
has weighted projective spaces
\mathbb{P}(1^{[r]},2^{[r]},\ldots,k^{[r]})
as fibers. Moreover, under the natural projection
\pi_{k}^{GG}\colon X_{k}^{GG}\to X
, one obtains the direct image formula

(2.16)

(\pi_{k}^{GG})_{\ast}\mathcal{O}_{X_{k}^{GG}}(m)\cong\mathcal{E}_{k,m}^{GG}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}

for all positive integers
k
and
m
.

2.5.
Invariant jet differentials

In geometric context,
(J_{k}V\setminus(X\times\{0\}))/\mathbb{C}^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\times$}}
is unwieldy, and we prefer
(J_{k}V\setminus\{0\})/\mathbb{G}_{k}
if such quotients would exist.
We will see that
X_{k}
plays the role of such a quotient.
First, we recall a canonical vector subbundle
E_{k,m}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}
of
E_{k,m}^{GG}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}
introduced by Demailly
[
Dem97
]
.
The corresponding sheaf
\mathcal{E}_{k,m}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}
is locally defined by

\mathcal{E}_{k,m}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}(U)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\{Q\in\mathcal{O}(p_{k,V}^{-1}(U))\mid Q(\varphi\cdot j_{k}f)=\varphi^{\prime}(0)^{m}Q(j_{k}f),\forall j_{k}f\in p_{k,V}^{-1}(U),\forall\varphi\in\mathbb{G}_{k}\},

for any open subset
U\subset X
. In other words,
E_{k,m}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}=(E_{k,m}^{GG}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}})^{\mathbb{G}_{k}^{\prime}}
is the set of invariants of
E_{k,m}^{GG}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}
with respect to the reparametrization action of
\mathbb{G}_{k}^{\prime}
. We call
\mathcal{E}_{k,m}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}
invariant jet differentials sheaf of order
k
and weighted degree
m
, and
E_{k,m}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}
invariant jet differentials bundle of order
k
and weighted degree
m
.

Seeing that
\mathcal{E}_{k,m}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}\subset\mathcal{E}_{k,m}^{GG}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}
, there are natural induced filtrations

F_{s}^{p}(\mathcal{E}_{k,m}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}})=\mathcal{E}_{k,m}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}\bigcap F_{s}^{p}(\mathcal{E}_{k,m}^{GG}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}})

on
\mathcal{E}_{k,m}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}
, for
1\leqslant s\leqslant k-1
,
0\leqslant p\leqslant m
, and the graded terms are

\Gr^{\bm{\ell}}(\mathcal{E}_{k,m}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}})=\mathcal{E}_{k,m}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}\bigcap\Gr^{\bm{\ell}}(\mathcal{E}_{k,m}^{GG}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}),

for
\bm{\ell}\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
such that
|\bm{\ell}|_{k}=m
.

Theorem 2.4

(Theorem 6.8,
[
Dem97
]
)
.

Suppose that
(X,V)
is a directed manifold such that
V
has rank
r\geqslant 2
. Let
\pi_{0,k}\colon X_{k}\to X
be a natural projection defined by (
2.9
), and let
J_{k}V^{\reg}
be the bundle of regular
k
-jets of maps
f\colon(\mathbb{C},0)\to X
, that is,
k
-jets
j_{k}f
such that
f^{\prime}(0)\neq 0
.

i)

The quotient
J_{k}V^{\reg}/\mathbb{G}_{k}
has the structure of a locally trivial bundle over
X
, and there is a holomorphic embedding
J_{k}V^{\reg}/\mathbb{G}_{k}\hookrightarrow X_{k}
over
X
, which identifies
J_{k}V^{\reg}/\mathbb{G}_{k}
with
X_{k}^{\reg}
. That is to say,

J_{k}V^{\reg}/\mathbb{G}_{k}\cong X_{k}^{\reg}.

ii)

The direct image sheaf
(\pi_{0,k})_{\ast}\mathcal{O}_{X_{k}}(m)
is identified with the sheaf of invariant jet differentials
\mathcal{E}_{k,m}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}
, namely,

(2.17)

(\pi_{0,k})_{\ast}\mathcal{O}_{X_{k}}(m)\cong\mathcal{E}_{k,m}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}.

iii)

For every
m>0
, the relative base locus of the linear system
|\mathcal{O}_{X_{k}}(m)|
is equal to the set
X_{k}^{\sing}
. Moreover,
\mathcal{O}_{X_{k}}(1)
is relatively big over
X
.

Let
\bm{a}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}
such that
\|\bm{a}\|=m
.
We then have a subsheaf
{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}}^{\bm{a}}\mathcal{E}_{k,m}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}\subset\mathcal{E}_{k,m}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}
, which consists of polynomials
Q(f^{\prime},\dots,f^{(k)})\in\mathcal{E}_{k,m}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}
involving only monomials
(f^{(\bullet)})^{\bm{\ell}}
such that

|\bm{\ell}|_{>s}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\ell_{s+1}+2\ell_{s+2}+\dots+(k-s)\ell_{k}\leqslant a_{s+1}+\dots+a_{k},

regardless of the choice of coordinate, for each integer
0\leqslant s\leqslant k-1
.
Apparently,
{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}}^{\bm{a}}\mathcal{E}_{k,m}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}
is a locally free subsheaf of
\mathcal{E}_{k,m}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}
over
X
, and the corresponding vector bundle
{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}}^{\bm{a}}E_{k,m}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}
is also a subbundle of
E_{k,m}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}
.
Likewise, there are natural induced filtrations

F_{s}^{p}({\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}}^{\bm{a}}\mathcal{E}_{k,m}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}})={\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}}^{\bm{a}}\mathcal{E}_{k,m}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}\bigcap F_{s}^{p}(\mathcal{E}_{k,m}^{GG}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}})

on
{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}}^{\bm{a}}\mathcal{E}_{k,m}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}
, for
1\leqslant s\leqslant k-1
,
0\leqslant p\leqslant m
, and the graded terms are

\Gr^{\bm{\ell}}({\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}}^{\bm{a}}\mathcal{E}_{k,m}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}})={\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}}^{\bm{a}}\mathcal{E}_{k,m}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}\bigcap\Gr^{\bm{\ell}}(\mathcal{E}_{k,m}^{GG}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}),

for
\bm{\ell}\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
such that
|\bm{\ell}|_{k}=m
.

According to Proposition 6.16 in
[
Dem97
]
, for any
k,m\geqslant 0
and any
\bm{a}
as above, one also has

(2.18)

(\pi_{0,k})_{\ast}\mathcal{O}_{X_{k}}(\bm{a})\cong{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}}^{\bm{a}}\mathcal{E}_{k,m}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}.

3.
Wronskians on Demailly-Semple jet towers

3.1.
Wronskian sections

In this subsection, we are going to recall the Wronskian sections on the Demailly-Semple jet tower (see
[
Bro17
]
).
Let us start with a local construction of Wronskians.

Let
(X,V)
be a complex directed manifold, and let
U
be an open subset of
X
. Brotbek
[
Bro17
]
constructed the Wronskian of
k+1
local holomorphic functions
\sigma_{0},\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{k}\in\mathcal{O}(U)
as follows:

W_{U}^{V}(\sigma_{0},\dots,\sigma_{k})\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\begin{vmatrix}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}^{[0]}\sigma_{0}&\cdots&\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}^{[0]}\sigma_{k}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}^{[k]}\sigma_{0}&\cdots&\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}^{[k]}\sigma_{k}\end{vmatrix}\in\mathcal{O}(p_{k,V}^{-1}(U)),

where
\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}^{[\ell]}=\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}_{U,V}^{[\ell]}
defined by (
2.3
).

For any positive integer
k
, set

\displaystyle k^{\prime}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=1+2+\dots+k=\frac{k(k+1)}{2},

\displaystyle\bm{a}^{k}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=(k,k-1,\dots,1)\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}.

Applying Faà Di Bruno’s formula, Brotbek
[
Bro17
]
verified that
W_{U}^{V}(\sigma_{0},\dots,\sigma_{k})\in\mathcal{E}_{k,k^{\prime}}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}(U)
.
More concretely, one easily checks that
W_{U}^{V}(\sigma_{0},\dots,\sigma_{k})(j_{k}f)
involves only monomials
(f^{(\bullet)})^{\bm{\ell}}
such that
|\bm{\ell}|_{>s}\leqslant a_{s+1}^{k}+\dots+a_{k}^{k}=\displaystyle\frac{(k-s)(k-s+1)}{2}
, for each integer
0\leqslant s\leqslant k-1
.
In other words,

W_{U}^{V}(\sigma_{0},\dots,\sigma_{k})\in{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}}^{\bm{a}^{k}}\!\mathcal{E}_{k,k^{\prime}}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}(U)\subset\mathcal{E}_{k,k^{\prime}}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}(U).

In addition, thinking of the variant of
W_{U}^{V}
as a vector
\begin{pmatrix}\sigma_{0}&\sigma_{1}&\dots&\sigma_{k}\end{pmatrix}
in
\mathcal{O}(U)^{k+1}
, we easily see that
W_{U}^{V}
is invariant under the action of
\mathrm{SL}(k+1,\mathbb{C})
on
\mathcal{O}(U)^{k+1}
.

Observe that the restriction morphism
\mathcal{O}(J_{k}X)\to\mathcal{O}(J_{k}V)
induces a quotient morphism
\res_{k,V}\colon\mathcal{E}_{k,\bullet}^{GG}T_{X}^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}\to\mathcal{E}_{k,\bullet}^{GG}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}
.
Obviously,
\res_{k,V}
also maps
\overline{F}^{\bm{a}}\mathcal{E}_{k,m}T_{X}^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}
into
\overline{F}^{\bm{a}}\mathcal{E}_{k,m}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}
, and we have

W_{U}^{V}(\sigma_{0},\dots,\sigma_{k})=\res_{k,V}\left(W_{U}^{T_{X}}(\sigma_{0},\dots,\sigma_{k})\right).

When no confusion can arise, we hereinafter prefer to write
W_{U}(\sigma_{0},\dots,\sigma_{k})
rather than
W_{U}^{V}(\sigma_{0},\dots,\sigma_{k})
.

Now, we will globalize this construction.
For a holomorphic line bundle
L
on
X
, take an open subset
U
, on which
L
can be trivialized.
Take a trivialization of
L_{\restriction U}
, which associates
\sigma_{U}\in\mathcal{O}(U)
to a global section
\sigma\in H^{0}(X,L)
.
Given global sections
\sigma_{0},\dots,\sigma_{k}\in H^{0}(X,L)
, we immediately get a locally defined invariant jet differential

W_{U}(\sigma_{0},\dots,\sigma_{k})\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=W_{U}(\sigma_{0,U},\dots,\sigma_{k,U})\in{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}}^{\bm{a}^{k}}\!\mathcal{E}_{k,k^{\prime}}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}(U)\subset\mathcal{E}_{k,k^{\prime}}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}(U).

Moreover, they glue together into a global section

W(\sigma_{0},\dots,\sigma_{k})\in H^{0}(X,{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}}^{\bm{a}^{k}}\!E_{k,k^{\prime}}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}\!\otimes L^{k+1})\subset H^{0}(X,E_{k,k^{\prime}}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}\!\otimes L^{k+1}).

Thanks to (
2.17
), there is a global section

\omega(\sigma_{0},\dots,\sigma_{k})\in H^{0}(X_{k},\mathcal{O}_{X_{k}}(k^{\prime})\otimes\pi_{0,k}^{\ast}L^{k+1})

corresponding to
W(\sigma_{0},\dots,\sigma_{k})
, that is, they satisfy the relation

(\pi_{0,k})_{\ast}\omega(\sigma_{0},\dots,\sigma_{k})=W(\sigma_{0},\dots,\sigma_{k}).

More precisely, following the proof of Theorem 6.8 in
[
Dem97
]
,

(3.1)

W(\sigma_{0},\dots,\sigma_{k})(j_{k}f_{\!+z_{0}})=\omega(\sigma_{0},\dots,\sigma_{k})(f_{[k]}(z_{0}))\cdot(f^{\prime}_{[k-1]}(z_{0}))^{k^{\prime}}

holds for any tangent curve
f
of
(X,V)
and every regular point
z_{0}
of
f
, where
f_{\!+z_{0}}(z)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=f(z+z_{0})
and
f_{[k-1]}^{\prime}
is defined by (
2.12
).
Likewise, there is also a global section

\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}(\sigma_{0},\dots,\sigma_{k})\in H^{0}(X_{k},\mathcal{O}_{X_{k}}(\bm{a}^{k})\otimes\pi_{0,k}^{\ast}L^{k+1})

corresponding to
W(\sigma_{0},\dots,\sigma_{k})
under the isomorphism (
2.18
).

According to (
2.11
), one has

\mathcal{O}_{X_{k}}(k^{\prime})=\mathcal{O}_{X_{k}}(\bm{a}^{k})\otimes\mathcal{O}_{X_{k}}(\bm{b}^{k}\cdot\varGamma),

where
\bm{b}^{k}=(b_{1},\dots,b_{k})\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}
such that
b_{j}=k+(k-1)+\dots+(k-j+1)=\dfrac{j(2k-j+1)}{2}
for each
1\leqslant j\leqslant k
,
and
\bm{b}^{k}\cdot\varGamma=\sum_{1\leqslant j\leqslant k-1}b_{j}\pi_{j+1,k}^{\ast}\varGamma_{j+1}
.
We conclude from (
2.17
) and (
2.18
) that the inclusion

{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}}^{\bm{a}^{k}}\!\mathcal{E}_{k,k^{\prime}}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}\lhook\joinrel\longrightarrow\mathcal{E}_{k,k^{\prime}}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}

induces the inclusion

\mathcal{O}_{X_{k}}(\bm{a}^{k})\lhook\joinrel\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{X_{k}}(k^{\prime}).

More concretely, the latter inclusion is written as

\displaystyle\sigma_{\bm{b}^{k}\cdot\varGamma}\otimes\colon\mathcal{O}_{X_{k}}(\bm{a}^{k})

\displaystyle\lhook\joinrel\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{X_{k}}(k^{\prime})

\displaystyle\xi

\displaystyle\longmapsto\sigma_{\bm{b}^{k}\cdot\varGamma}(\pi(\xi))\otimes\xi,

for a canonical section
\sigma_{\bm{b}^{k}\cdot\varGamma}
of
\mathcal{O}_{X_{k}}(\bm{b}^{k}\cdot\varGamma)
, i.e.,
(\sigma_{\bm{b}^{k}\cdot\varGamma})=\bm{b}^{k}\cdot\varGamma
,
where
\pi\colon\mathcal{O}_{X_{k}}(\bm{a}^{k})\to X_{k}
is the bundle projection.
It amounts to saying that

(3.2)

\omega(\sigma_{0},\dots,\sigma_{k})=\sigma_{\bm{b}^{k}\cdot\varGamma}\cdot\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}(\sigma_{0},\dots,\sigma_{k}),

for any global sections
\sigma_{0},\dots,\sigma_{k}\in H^{0}(X,L)
.

3.2.
Wronskian ideal sheaf

In this subsection, we are going to generalize the Wronskian ideal sheaf in
[
Bro17
]
to linear systems.
Let us recall the linear system and the related terminology.

Let
X
be a projective manifold, and choose a divisor
D_{0}
on
X
.
A complete linear system
|D_{0}|
on
X
is defined as the set (maybe empty) of all effective divisors linearly equivalent to
D_{0}
, namely,

|D_{0}|\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\{D\geqslant 0\mid D\sim D_{0}\}.

Let
L=\mathcal{O}_{X}(D_{0})
be a holomorphic line bundle on
X
.
It is evident that
|D_{0}|
is in one-to-one corresponding with
(H^{0}(X,L)\setminus\{0\})/\mathbb{C}^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\times$}}
, that is,
|D_{0}|\cong\textnormal{P}(H^{0}(X,L))
.
This implies that
|D_{0}|
merely depends on the line bundle
L
associated to
D
.
Thus
|L|\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=|D_{0}|
is well-defined.

Furthermore, a subset
\mathfrak{S}\subset|D_{0}|
is called a linear system if it is a linear subspace for the projective space structure of
|D_{0}|
,
which amounts to saying that
\mathfrak{S}
corresponds to a linear subspace
\mathbb{S}\subset H^{0}(X,L)
, where
\mathbb{S}=\{\sigma\in H^{0}(X,L)\mid(\sigma)\in\mathfrak{S}\}\cup\{0\}
.
Obviously,
\mathfrak{S}\cong\textnormal{P}(\mathbb{S})
.

A point
x\in X
is said to be a base point of a linear system
\mathfrak{S}
if
x\in\Supp D
for all
D\in\mathfrak{S}
.
The set of all the base points of
\mathfrak{S}

\Bs(\mathfrak{S})\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\{x\in X\mid x\in\Supp D,\,\forall\,D\in\mathfrak{S}\}

is referred to as the base locus of
\mathfrak{S}
.
What’s more, a linear system
\mathfrak{S}
is said to be base point free, if
\Bs(\mathfrak{S})=\varnothing
, i.e., for every
x\in X
, there exists
D\in\mathfrak{S}
such that
x\notin\Supp D
.

Let us recall the base ideal of a linear system
\mathfrak{S}\subset|L|
on
X
.
Consider the evaluation map

\mathrm{ev}\colon\mathbb{S}\longrightarrow L,

where
\mathbb{S}\subset H^{0}(X,L)
is the linear subspace corresponding to
\mathfrak{S}
.
Then

\mathfrak{s}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\mathrm{im}(\mathrm{ev})\otimes L^{-1}\subset\mathcal{O}_{X}

is called the base ideal of
\mathfrak{S}
.
It is evident that
\mathfrak{s}=\mathcal{O}_{X}
if and only if
\mathfrak{S}
is base point free.

Take a directed projective manifold
(X,V)
, and choose a non-empty linear system
\mathfrak{S}\subset|L|
on
X
with the corresponding subspace
\mathbb{S}\subset H^{0}(X,L)
.
Set

\mathbb{W}(X_{k},\mathfrak{S})\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\Span\{\omega(\sigma_{0},\dots,\sigma_{k})\mid\sigma_{0},\dots,\sigma_{k}\in\mathbb{S}\},

which is a linear subspace of
H^{0}(X_{k},\mathcal{O}_{X_{k}}(k^{\prime})\otimes\pi_{0,k}^{\ast}L^{k+1})
.
We denote by
\mathfrak{W}(X_{k},\mathfrak{S})
the corresponding linear system, i.e.,

\mathfrak{W}(X_{k},\mathfrak{S})\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\{(\omega)\mid\omega\neq 0,\,\omega\in\mathbb{W}(X_{k},\mathfrak{S})\}.

Therefore, we define the
k
-th Wronskian ideal sheaf of
\mathfrak{S}
to be the base ideal of
\mathfrak{W}(X_{k},\mathfrak{S})
, and denote it by
\mathfrak{w}(X_{k},\mathfrak{S})
.

By convention, if
\mathfrak{S}=|L|
is a complete linear system, we simply write
\mathbb{W}(X_{k},L)
,
\mathfrak{W}(X_{k},L)
and
\mathfrak{w}(X_{k},L)
rather than
\mathbb{W}(X_{k},\mathfrak{S})
,
\mathfrak{W}(X_{k},\mathfrak{S})
and
\mathfrak{w}(X_{k},\mathfrak{S})
, respectively.
Obviously, our definition of
\mathfrak{w}(X_{k},\mathfrak{S})
generalizes the
k
-th Wronskian ideal sheaf of
L
, which is introduced by Brotbek
[
Bro17
]
.

In addition, set

\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}(X_{k},\mathfrak{S})\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\Span\{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}(\sigma_{0},\dots,\sigma_{k})\mid\sigma_{0},\dots,\sigma_{k}\in\mathbb{S}\}

as a linear subspace of
H^{0}(X_{k},\mathcal{O}_{X_{k}}(\bm{a}^{k})\otimes\pi_{0,k}^{\ast}L^{k+1})
.
Likewise, we denote the corresponding linear system by
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}(X_{k},\mathfrak{S})
, and denote the base ideal of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}(X_{k},\mathfrak{S})
by
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}(X_{k},\mathfrak{S})
.
It follows from (
3.2
) that

\displaystyle\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}(X_{k},\mathfrak{S})

\displaystyle=\{D\mid D+\bm{b}^{k}\cdot\varGamma\in\mathfrak{W}(X_{k},\mathfrak{S})\},

\displaystyle\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}(X_{k},\mathfrak{S})

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{w}(X_{k},\mathfrak{S})\cdot\mathcal{O}_{X_{k}}(\bm{b}^{k}\cdot\varGamma).

In particular,
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}(X_{1},\mathfrak{S})=\mathfrak{W}(X_{1},\mathfrak{S})
and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}(X_{1},\mathfrak{S})=\mathfrak{w}(X_{1},\mathfrak{S})
.

We say that
\mathfrak{S}
separates
k
-jets at a point
x\in X
if the evaluation map

\mathbb{S}\longrightarrow L\otimes\mathcal{O}_{X,x}/\mathfrak{m}_{X,x}^{k+1}

is surjective.
Analogue to Lemma 2.4 in
[
Bro17
]
, we have the following lemma.

Lemma 3.1
.

If
\mathfrak{S}
separates
k
-jets at each point of
X
, then

\Supp(\mathcal{O}_{X_{k}}/\mathfrak{w}(X_{k},\mathfrak{S}))=X_{k}^{\sing}.

In other words, the base locus
\Bs(\mathfrak{W}(X_{k},\mathfrak{S}))=X_{k}^{\sing}
.

What’s more, analogue to Lemma 2.8 in
[
Bro17
]
, we see that
\mathfrak{w}(X_{k},\mathfrak{S})
is independent of the choice of the linear system
\mathfrak{S}
, provided that
\mathfrak{S}
separates
k
-jets at each point of
X
.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
\mathfrak{S}
be a linear system that separates
k
-jets at each point of
X
.
For any
x\in X
and any
x_{k}\in X_{k}
such that
\pi_{0,k}(x_{k})=x
, one has

\mathfrak{w}(X_{k},\mathfrak{S})_{x_{k}}=\big(\omega_{x_{k}}(f_{0},\dots,f_{k})\big)_{f_{0},\dots,f_{k}\in\mathcal{O}_{X,x}}\subset\mathcal{O}_{X_{k},x_{k}},

where the right hand side denotes the ideal whose generators are
\omega_{x_{k}}(f_{0},\dots,f_{k})
for all
f_{0},\dots,f_{k}\in\mathcal{O}_{X,x}
.

Therefore, we can define the asymptotic Wronskian ideal sheaf of
X_{k}
by

\mathfrak{w}_{\infty}(X_{k})\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\mathfrak{w}(X_{k},\mathfrak{S})\subset\mathcal{O}_{X_{k}},

for any linear system
\mathfrak{S}
that separates
k
-jets at each point of
X
.

4.
Logarithmic jets and jet differentials

4.1.
Logarithmic jet spaces

Let
X
be a complex manifold of dimension
n
with a normal crossing divisor
D
.
This amounts to saying that for each point
x\in X
, one can take an open subset
U\subset X
containing
x
with local coordinates
z_{1},\dots,z_{n}
centered at
x
such that
D
is defined by
z_{1}z_{2}\cdots z_{\ell}=0
in
U
for some
\ell\leqslant n
dependent on
x
.
Then such a pair
(X,D)
is called a logarithmic manifold.
The logarithmic cotangent sheaf
\mathcal{T}_{X}^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}(\log D)
of
(X,D)
is a locally free subsheaf of the sheaf of meromorphic
1
-form on
X
, whose localization at any point
x\in X
is given by

\mathcal{T}_{X,x}^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}(\log D)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\mathcal{O}_{X,x}\frac{\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}z_{i}}{z_{i}}+\sum_{j=\ell+1}^{n}\mathcal{O}_{X,x}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}z_{j}.

Seeing that
\ell=0
when
x\notin D
, this local expression implies that the restriction of
\mathcal{T}_{X}^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}(\log D)
on
X\setminus D
is identified with
\mathcal{T}_{X\setminus D}^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}
.
The vector bundle associated to
\mathcal{T}_{X}^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}(\log D)
is denoted by
T_{X}^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}(\log D)
and called the logarithmic cotangent bundle along
D
.
And its dual is denoted by
