T_{X}(-\log D)
, and called the logarithmic tangent bundle along
D
.
The corresponding sheaf
\mathcal{T}_{X}(-\log D)
is a local free subsheaf of the holomorphic tangent sheaf
\mathcal{T}_{X}
, whose localization at any point
x\in X
is given by

\mathcal{T}_{X,x}(-\log D)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\mathcal{O}_{X,x}z_{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{i}}+\sum_{j=\ell+1}^{n}\mathcal{O}_{X,x}\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{j}}.

Obviously, its restriction on
X\setminus D
is identified with
\mathcal{T}_{X\setminus D}
.

Given an open subset
U\subset X
, now consider holomorphic sections
\alpha\in H^{0}(U,J_{k}X)
.
One says that
\alpha
is a logarithmic
k
-jet field along
D
if
\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}^{[j]}\omega(\alpha_{\restriction U^{\prime}})
are holomorphic for all
\omega\in\mathcal{T}_{X}^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}(\log D)(U^{\prime})
and
0\leqslant j\leqslant k-1
, for all open subset
U^{\prime}\subset U
.
Here
\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}^{[j]}=\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}_{U^{\prime},T_{X}}^{[j]}
defined by (
2.1
), which is also well-defined for meromorphic
1
-forms
\omega
.
One denotes by
\mathcal{J}_{k}(X,-\log D)
the sheaf, which locally consists of germs of these logarithmic
k
-jet fields
\alpha
, and calls it logarithmic
k
-jet sheaf along
D
.
By
[
Nog86
]
(see also Section 4.6.3 of
[
NW14
]
),
\mathcal{J}_{k}(X,-\log D)
is the sheaf of sections of a holomorphic fiber bundle
J_{k}(X,-\log D)
over
X
.
More precisely, there is a fiber map

\tau\colon J_{k}(X,-\log D)\longrightarrow J_{k}X,

such that the induced map of sections

\tau_{\ast}\colon H^{0}(U,J_{k}(X,-\log D))\longrightarrow\mathcal{J}_{k}(X,-\log D)(U)

is isomorphism for any open subset
U\subset X
.
One refers to
J_{k}(X,-\log D)
as the logarithmic
k
-jet bundle of
(X,D)
.
Likewise, its restriction on
X\setminus D
is identified with
J_{k}(X\setminus D)
.

Following
[
DL01
]
, a logarithmic directed manifold is a triple
(X,D,V(-\log D))
consisting of a logarithmic manifold
(X,D)
equipped with a holomorphic subbundle
V(-\log D)\subset T_{X}(-\log D)
.
It is evident that
V^{\circ}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=V(-\log D)_{\restriction X\setminus D}
is a holomorphic subbundle of
T_{X\setminus D}
.
Seeing that
J_{k}V^{\circ}\subset J_{k}(X\setminus D)\cong J_{k}(X,-\log D)_{\restriction X\setminus D}\subset J_{k}(X,-\log D)
,
J_{k}V^{\circ}
can be think of as a subset of
J_{k}(X,-\log D)
.
Thereupon one defines logarithmic jet spaces
J_{k}V(-\log D)
over
(X,D,V(-\log D))
to be the (Euclidean) closure of
J_{k}V^{\circ}
in
J_{k}(X,-\log D)
.

Indeed, the natural projection map

(4.1)

p_{k,V\!(\!-\!\log\!D)}\colon J_{k}V(-\log D)\longrightarrow X

gives a
\mathbb{C}^{rk}
-fiber bundle over
X
, where
r=\rank(V(-\log D))
.
Let
V\subset T_{X}
be the closure of
V^{\circ}
in
T_{X}
, then one sees that
V
is also a holomorphic subbundle of
T_{X}
.
Hence
\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}_{U,V}^{[j]}
is well-defined for every local sections
\omega\in\mathcal{V}^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}\!(\log D)
, where
\mathcal{V}^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}(\log D)
is the dual of
\mathcal{V}(-\log D)
.
Moreover, for any local section
\alpha\in\mathcal{J}_{k}V(-\log D)
, one has
\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}_{U,V}^{[\ell]}(\res_{V\!(\!-\!\log\!D)}\omega)(\alpha)=\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}_{U,T_{X}}^{[\ell]}\omega(\alpha)
, where
\res_{V}\colon\mathcal{T}_{X}^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}(\log D)\to\mathcal{V}^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}(\log D)
is a quotient morphism induced by the restriction morphism
\mathcal{O}(T_{X}(-\log D))\to\mathcal{O}(V(-\log D))
.
Take an open subset
U\subset X
such that there exist
r
local sections
\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{r}\in\mathcal{V}^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}\!(\log D)(U)
generating
V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}\!(\log D)\subset T_{X}^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}(\log D)
at every point of
U
.
Analogue to (
2.2
), one has a local trivialization of
p_{k,V\!(\!-\!\log\!D)}^{-1}(U)
given by

(4.2)

p_{k,V\!(\!-\!\log\!D)}\times(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}_{U,V}^{[\ell]}\omega_{i})_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leqslant i\leqslant r\hskip 8.19447pt\\
0\leqslant\ell\leqslant k-1\end{subarray}}\colon p_{k,V\!(\!-\!\log\!D)}^{-1}(U)\longrightarrow U\times\mathbb{C}^{rk}.

Remark 4.1
.

In the absolute case
V(-\log D)=T_{X}(-\log D)
, one has a canonical choice of
\omega_{i}
as follows:

\omega_{1}=\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\log z_{1},\,\dots,\,\omega_{\ell}=\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\log z_{\ell},\,\omega_{\ell+1}=\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}z_{\ell+1},\,\dots,\,\omega_{n}=\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}z_{n},

where
z_{1},\cdots,z_{n}
is the local coordinate of
U
, given by the definition of the normal crossing divisor
D
.
In general, given a holomorphic subbundle
V(-\log D)\subset T_{X}(-\log D)
with rank
r
, after permuting
z_{1},\dots,z_{\ell}
and
z_{\ell+1},\dots,z_{n}
, respectively,
one has a system of generators
\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{p},\omega_{\ell+1},\dots,\omega_{\ell+q}\in\mathcal{V}^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}\!(\log D)(U)
,
where
0\leqslant p\leqslant\ell
,
0\leqslant q\leqslant n-\ell
and
p+q=r
.

4.2.
Logarithmic Demailly-Semple jet towers

In
[
DL01
]
, Dethloff and Lu generalized Demailly-Semple jet tower to the logarithmic case.
Given a logarithmic directed manifold
(X,D,V(-\log D))
, let us start from
(X_{0}(D),D_{0},V_{0}(-\log D_{0}))=(X,D,V)
.
A sequence of logarithmic directed manifolds
(X_{\bullet}(D),D_{\bullet},V_{\bullet}(-\log D_{\bullet}))
is inductively constructed as follows.

Let
X_{k}(D)=\textnormal{P}(V_{k-1}(-\log D_{k-1}))\allowbreak\cong\mathbb{P}(V_{k-1}^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}\!(\log D_{k-1}))
with its natural projection
\bar{\pi}_{k}
to
X_{k-1}(D)
, and let
D_{k}=\bar{\pi}_{k}^{-1}(D_{k-1})
, for each
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
.
Observe that the differential of
\bar{\pi}_{k}
,
\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\bar{\pi}_{k}\colon T_{X_{k}(D)}\to\bar{\pi}_{k}^{\ast}T_{X_{k-1}(D)}
behaves well as a differential map
T_{X_{k}(D)}(-\log D_{k})\to\bar{\pi}_{k}^{\ast}T_{X_{k-1}(D)}(-\log D_{k-1})
.
One defines

V_{k}(-\log D_{k})\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\bar{\pi}_{k})^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{X_{k}(D)}(-1),

where
\mathcal{O}_{X_{k}(D)}(-1)
is the tautological line bundle of
\textnormal{P}(V_{k-1}(-\log D_{k-1}))
and regarded as the subbundle of
\bar{\pi}_{k}^{\ast}V_{k-1}(-\log D_{k-1})
.
More concretely, for every point
(x,[v])\in X_{k}(D)=\textnormal{P}(V_{k-1}(-\log D_{k-1}))
associated with a logarithmic tangent vector
v\in(V_{k-1}(-\log D_{k-1}))_{x}\setminus\{0\}
for
x\in X_{k-1}
, one defines the logarithmic tangent subbundle
V_{k}(-\log D_{k})\subset T_{X_{k}(D)}(-\log D_{k})
by

(V_{k}(-\log D_{k}))_{(x,[v])}=\{\xi\in(T_{X_{k}(D)}(-\log D_{k}))_{(x,[v])}\mid\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\bar{\pi}_{k}(\xi)\in\mathbb{C}\,v\},

where
\mathbb{C}\,v\subset(V_{k-1}(-\log D_{k-1}))_{x}
.
Then the logarithmic directed manifold
(X_{k}(D),\allowbreak D_{k},V_{k}(-\log D_{k}))
is called the logarithmic Demailly-Semple
k
-jet tower of
(X,D,\allowbreak V(-\log D))
.
When no confusion can arise, one simply writes
(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}_{k},D_{k},\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{V}_{k})
in what follows.

Next, we are going to show that the logarithmic Demailly-Semple jet tower has similar properties to the Demailly-Semple jet tower.

The bundle
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{V}_{k}
is characterized by the two exact sequences:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}_{k}/\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}_{k-1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{V}_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}_{k}}(-1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\bar{\pi}_{k})_{\ast}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}_{k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{\pi}_{k}^{\ast}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{V}_{k-1}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}_{k}}(1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}_{k}/\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}_{k-1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}

Moreover, for each
k\geqslant 2
, the composition of vector bundle morphisms over
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}_{k}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}_{k}}(-1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{\pi}_{k}^{\ast}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{V}_{k-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{\pi}_{k}^{\ast}\mathcal{O}_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}_{k-1}}(-1)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{\pi}_{k}^{\ast}(\bar{\pi}_{k-1})_{\ast}}

admits
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{k}\subset\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}_{k}
as its zero divisor,
which is effective and satisfies that

(4.3)

\mathcal{O}_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}_{k}}(1)=\bar{\pi}_{k}^{\ast}\mathcal{O}_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}_{k-1}}(1)\otimes\mathcal{O}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{k}).

For the sake of convenience, for each
0\leqslant j\leqslant k-1
, one writes

(4.4)

\bar{\pi}_{j,k}=\bar{\pi}_{j+1}\circ\bar{\pi}_{j}\circ\dots\circ\bar{\pi}_{k}\colon\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}_{k}\longrightarrow\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}_{j},

and

\mathcal{O}_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}_{k}}(\bm{a})\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\bar{\pi}_{1,k}^{\ast}\mathcal{O}_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}_{1}}(a_{1})\otimes\bar{\pi}_{2,k}^{\ast}\mathcal{O}_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}_{2}}(a_{2})\otimes\dots\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}_{k}}(a_{k}),

for every
\bm{a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k})\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}
.
Likewise, it follows from (
4.3
) that

(4.5)

\mathcal{O}_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}_{k}}(\bm{a})=\mathcal{O}_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}_{k}}(b_{k})\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}_{k}}(-\bm{b}\cdot\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}),

where

\displaystyle\bm{b}

\displaystyle=(b_{1},\dots,b_{k})\in\mathbb{Z}^{k},\quad b_{j}=a_{1}+\dots+a_{j},

\displaystyle\bm{b}

\displaystyle\cdot\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}=\sum_{1\leqslant j\leqslant k-1}b_{j}\bar{\pi}_{j+1,k}^{\ast}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{j+1}.

Furthermore, one defines

\displaystyle\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}_{k}^{\reg}

\displaystyle=\bigcap_{2\leqslant j\leqslant k}\bar{\pi}_{j,k}^{-1}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}_{j}\setminus\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{j}),

\displaystyle\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}_{k}^{\sing}

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{2\leqslant j\leqslant k}\bar{\pi}_{j,k}^{-1}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{j})=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}_{k}\setminus\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}_{k}^{\reg}.

4.3.
Logarithmic jet differentials

Let
(X,D,V(-\log D))
be a logarithmic directed manifold, and take an open subset
U\subset X
. A local meromorphic
k
-jet differential
Q
on
U
is called a logarithmic
k
-jet differential,
if
Q(\alpha)
is holomorphic for any logarithmic
k
-jet field
\alpha\in\mathcal{J}_{k}V(-\log D)(U)
,
where
\mathcal{J}_{k}V(-\log D)\subset\mathcal{J}_{k}(X,-\log D)
is the sheaf of sections of
J_{k}V(-\log D)
over
X
.
It amounts to saying that
\tau^{\ast}Q
is a homogeneous polynomial on
p_{k,V\!(\!-\!\log\!D)}^{-1}(U)
.
One denotes the sheaf of logarithmic
k
-jet differential by
\mathcal{E}_{k,\bullet}^{GG}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}\!(\log D)
.
There is a natural splitting

\mathcal{E}_{k,\bullet}^{GG}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}\!(\log D)=\bigoplus_{m\geqslant 0}\mathcal{E}_{k,m}^{GG}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}\!(\log D),

where
\mathcal{E}_{k,m}^{GG}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}\!(\log D)
is locally defined by

\mathcal{E}_{k,m}^{GG}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}\!(\log D)(U)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\{Q\in\mathcal{E}_{k,\bullet}^{GG}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}\!(\log D)(U)\mid Q_{\restriction U\setminus D}\in\mathcal{E}_{k,m}^{GG}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}(U\setminus D)\},

called logarithmic Green-Griffiths jet differential sheaf of order
k
and weighted degree
m
.
Likewise, logarithmic invariant jet differential sheaf
\mathcal{E}_{k,m}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}\!(\log D)
of order
k
and weighted degree
m
is locally defined by

\mathcal{E}_{k,m}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}\!(\log D)(U)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\{Q\in\mathcal{E}_{k,\bullet}^{GG}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}\!(\log D)(U)\mid Q_{\restriction U\setminus D}\in\mathcal{E}_{k,m}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}(U\setminus D)\}.

More precisely,
\mathcal{E}_{k,m}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}\!(\log D)
is a locally free sheaf, which consists of logarithmic
k
-jet differentials
Q\in\mathcal{E}_{k,\bullet}^{GG}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}\!(\log D)(U)
such that

Q(\varphi\cdot j_{k}f)=\varphi^{\prime}(0)^{m}Q(j_{k}f),

for any
\varphi\in\mathbb{G}_{k}
and any
j_{k}f\in p_{k,V^{\circ}}^{-1}(U\setminus D)\cap J_{k}V^{\circ\reg}
.

What’s more, we define
J_{k}V(-\log D)^{\sing}
the closure of
J_{k}V^{\circ\sing}
in
J_{k}V(-\log D)
, and
J_{k}V(-\log D)^{\reg}=J_{k}V(-\log D)\setminus J_{k}V(-\log D)^{\sing}
, where
J_{k}V^{\circ\sing}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=J_{k}V^{\circ}\setminus J_{k}V^{\circ\reg}
is the set of singular
k
-jets over
(X\setminus D,V^{\circ})
.

Theorem 4.2
.

Suppose that
(X,D,V(-\log D))
is a logarithmic directed manifold such that
V(-\log D)
has rank
r\geqslant 2
. Let
\bar{\pi}_{0,k}\colon X_{k}(D)\to X
be a natural projection defined by (
4.4
).

i)

The quotient
J_{k}V(-\log D)^{\reg}/\mathbb{G}_{k}
has the structure of a locally trivial bundle over
X
, and there is a holomorphic embedding
J_{k}V(-\log D)^{\reg}/\mathbb{G}_{k}\hookrightarrow X_{k}(D)
over
X
, which identifies
J_{k}V(-\log D)^{\reg}/\mathbb{G}_{k}
with
X_{k}(D)^{\reg}
. That is to say,

J_{k}V(-\log D)^{\reg}/\mathbb{G}_{k}\cong X_{k}(D)^{\reg}.

ii)

The direct image sheaf
(\bar{\pi}_{0,k})_{\ast}\mathcal{O}_{X_{k}(D)}(m)
is identified with the sheaf of logarithmic invariant jet differentials
\mathcal{E}_{k,m}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}\!(\log D)
, namely,

(4.6)

(\bar{\pi}_{0,k})_{\ast}\mathcal{O}_{X_{k}(D)}(m)\cong\mathcal{E}_{k,m}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}\!(\log D).

iii)

For every
m>0
, the relative base locus of the linear system
|\mathcal{O}_{X_{k}(D)}(m)|
lies in the set
X_{k}(D)^{\sing}\cup\bar{\pi}_{0,k}^{-1}(D)
. Moreover,
\mathcal{O}_{X_{k}(D)}(1)
is relatively big over
X
.

Proof.

i) See Proposition 3.8, c) in
[
DL01
]
.

ii) See Proposition 3.9 in
[
DL01
]
.

iii) Let
U
be an affine open subset of
X
.
By definition, every
Q\in\mathcal{E}_{k,m}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}\!(\log D)(U)
is a local meromorphic section of
E_{k,m}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}(U)
, which is holomorphic on
U\setminus D
.
Conversely, for each
Q\in\mathcal{E}_{k,m}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}(U)
, we see that
\tau^{\ast}Q
is indeed a local section of
E_{k,m}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}\!(\log D)
, which vanishes on
D
.
Seeing that
X_{k}(D)^{\sing}\cap\bar{\pi}_{0,k}^{-1}(X\setminus D)=(X\setminus D)_{k}^{\sing}
, the result follows from Theorem
2.4
,
iii
).
∎

5.
Logarithmic Wronskians

In
[
BD19
]
, Brotbek and Deng generalize the Wronskian sections and the Wronskian ideal sheaf to the logarithmic case.
In this section, we are going to further generalize these concepts.

5.1.
Logarithmic Wronskian sections

Let
(X,D,V(-\log D))
be a logarithmic directed manifold, and take an open subset
U\subset X
.
For each
k\geqslant 0
, Brotbek and Deng
[
BD19
]
define a
\mathbb{C}
-linear map
\nabla_{D}^{[k]}\colon\mathcal{O}_{X}(D)(U)\to\mathcal{E}_{k,k}^{GG}T_{X}^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}\!(\log D)(U)
given by

\nabla_{D}^{[\ell]}\sigma=\sigma_{D}\cdot\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}_{U,T_{X}}^{[\ell]}\!\left(\frac{\sigma}{\sigma_{D}\!}\right),

where
\sigma_{D}
is a canonical section of
\mathcal{O}_{X}(D)
.
Hence we construct the logarithmic Wronskian of a local holomorphic function
\sigma_{0}\in\mathcal{O}(U)
and
k
local holomorphic sections
\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{k}\in H^{0}(U,\mathcal{O}_{X}(D))
as follows:

\displaystyle W_{D,U}^{V\!(\!-\!\log\!D)}(\sigma_{0};\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{k})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=

\displaystyle\begin{vmatrix}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}_{U,V}^{[0]}\sigma_{0}&\nabla_{D}^{[0]}\sigma_{1}&\cdots&\nabla_{D}^{[0]}\sigma_{k}\\
\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}_{U,V}^{[1]}\sigma_{0}&\nabla_{D}^{[1]}\sigma_{1}&\cdots&\nabla_{D}^{[1]}\sigma_{k}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}_{U,V}^{[k]}\sigma_{0}&\nabla_{D}^{[k]}\sigma_{1}&\cdots&\nabla_{D}^{[k]}\sigma_{k}\end{vmatrix}\in\mathcal{E}_{k,k^{\prime}}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}\!(\log D)\otimes\mathcal{O}_{X}(kD)(U),

where
V\subset T_{X}
is the (Euclidean) closure of
V^{\circ}
in
T_{X}
and we abusively write

\nabla_{D}^{[\ell]}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\res_{k,V\!(\!-\!\log\!D)}\circ\nabla_{D\restriction U}^{[\ell]}=\sigma_{D}\cdot\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}_{U,V}^{[\ell]}\!\left(\frac{\sigma}{\sigma_{D}\!}\right).

Here
\res_{k,V\!(\!-\!\log\!D)}\colon\mathcal{E}_{k,\bullet}^{GG}T_{X}^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}\!(\log D)\to\mathcal{E}_{k,\bullet}^{GG}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}\!(\log D)
is a quotient morphism induced by the restriction morphism
\mathcal{O}(J_{k}(X,-\log D))\to\mathcal{O}(J_{k}V(-\log D))
.
Moreover, we see that

W_{D,U}^{V\!(\!-\!\log\!D)}(\sigma_{0};\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{k})=\res_{k,V\!(\!-\!\log\!D)}\left(W_{D,U}^{T\!_{X}\!(\!-\!\log\!D)}(\sigma_{0};\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{k})\right).

When no confusion can arise, we hereinafter prefer to write
W_{D,U}(\sigma_{0};\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{k})
rather than
W_{D,U}^{V\!(\!-\!\log\!D)}\allowbreak(\sigma_{0};\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{k})
.

Furthermore, our construction of the logarithmic Wronskian is related to the Wronskian in the following way.

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
(X,D,V(-\log D))
be a logarithmic directed manifold, and take an open subset
U\subset X
.
For any
\sigma_{D}\in\mathcal{O}(U)
and any
\sigma_{0},\dots,\sigma_{k}\in H^{0}(U,\mathcal{O}_{X}(D))
, we have

W_{U}^{V}(\sigma_{D}\cdot\sigma_{0},\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{k})=\sigma_{D}\cdot W_{D,U}^{V\!(\!-\!\log\!D)}(\sigma_{0};\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{k}),

where
V\subset T_{X}
is the closure of
V^{\circ}
in
T_{X}
.

Proof.

For any
\sigma\in H^{0}(U,\mathcal{O}_{X}(D))
, using the Leibniz rule to
\sigma_{D}\cdot\dfrac{\sigma}{\sigma_{D}\!}
, we get

\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}_{U,V}^{[\ell]}\sigma=\sum_{i=0}^{\ell}\binom{k}{i}\frac{\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}_{U,V}^{[\ell-i]}\sigma_{D}}{\sigma_{D}\!}\nabla_{D}^{[i]}\sigma.

Then it is evident that

\displaystyle W_{U}^{V}(\sigma_{D}\cdot\sigma_{0},\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{k})

\displaystyle=\begin{vmatrix}\nabla_{D}^{[0]}(\sigma_{D}\cdot\sigma_{0})&\nabla_{D}^{[0]}\sigma_{1}&\cdots&\nabla_{D}^{[0]}\sigma_{k}\\
\nabla_{D}^{[1]}(\sigma_{D}\cdot\sigma_{0})&\nabla_{D}^{[1]}\sigma_{1}&\cdots&\nabla_{D}^{[1]}\sigma_{k}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\nabla_{D}^{[k]}(\sigma_{D}\cdot\sigma_{0})&\nabla_{D}^{[k]}\sigma_{1}&\cdots&\nabla_{D}^{[k]}\sigma_{k}\end{vmatrix}

\displaystyle=\sigma_{D}\cdot W_{D,U}^{V\!(\!-\!\log\!D)}(\sigma_{0};\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{k}).\qed

For a holomorphic line bundle
L
on
X
, take an open subset
U
, on which both
L\otimes\mathcal{O}_{X}(-D)
and
\mathcal{O}_{X}(D)
can be trivialized.
Choose a trivialization of
L\otimes\mathcal{O}_{X}(-D)_{\restriction U}
and a trivialization of
\mathcal{O}_{X}(D)_{\restriction U}
.
Observe that the trivialization of
L\otimes\mathcal{O}_{X}(-D)_{\restriction U}
induces a map
H^{0}(X,L)\to H^{0}(U,\mathcal{O}_{X}(D))
,
which associates
\sigma_{U}^{D}=\left(\dfrac{\sigma}{\sigma_{D}\!}\right)_{U\setminus D}\!\!\sigma_{D}
to a global section
\sigma\in H^{0}(X,L)
,
where
\left(\dfrac{\sigma}{\sigma_{D}\!}\right)_{U\setminus D}\!\!\in\mathcal{O}(U\setminus D)
is a meromorphic function on
U
corresponding to
\dfrac{\sigma}{\sigma_{D}\!}\in H^{0}(X\setminus D,L\otimes\mathcal{O}_{X}(-D))
under the trivialization of
L\otimes\mathcal{O}_{X}(-D)_{\restriction U}
.
Given global sections
\sigma_{0}\in H^{0}(X,L\otimes\mathcal{O}_{X}(-D))
and
\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{k}\in H^{0}(X,L)
, we thus obtain a locally defined invariant jet differential

W_{D,U}(\sigma_{0};\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{k})\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=W_{U}(\sigma_{0,U};\sigma_{1,U}^{D},\dots,\sigma_{k,U}^{D})\in\mathcal{E}_{k,k^{\prime}}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}\!(\log D)(U).

It follows from Lemma
5.1
that

(5.1)

W_{D,U}(\sigma_{0};\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{k})=\sigma_{D}^{-1}\cdot W_{U}(\sigma_{D}\cdot\sigma_{0},\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{k}),

where the Wronskian on the right-hand side is under the trivialization of
L_{\restriction U}
given by the composition of the trivialization of
L\otimes\mathcal{O}_{X}(-D)_{\restriction U}
and
\mathcal{O}_{X}(D)_{\restriction U}
.
In addition, these logarithmic Wronskians glue together into a global section

W_{D}(\sigma_{0};\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{k})\in H^{0}(X,E_{k,k^{\prime}}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}\!(\log D)\otimes L^{k+1}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{X}(-D)).

What’s more, from (
5.1
), we easily get the following proposition.

Proposition 5.2
.

Let
(X,D,V(-\log D))
be a logarithmic directed manifold.
For any
\sigma_{0}\in H^{0}(X,L\otimes\mathcal{O}_{X}(-D))
and any
\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{k}\in H^{0}(X,L)
, one has

W(\sigma_{D}\cdot\sigma_{0},\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{k})=\sigma_{D}\cdot W_{D}(\sigma_{0};\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{k}).

Thanks to (
4.6
), there is a global section

\omega_{D}(\sigma_{0};\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{k})\in H^{0}(X_{k}(D),\mathcal{O}_{X_{k}(D)}(k^{\prime})\otimes\bar{\pi}_{0,k}^{\ast}(L^{k+1}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{X}(-D)))

corresponding to
W_{D}(\sigma_{0};\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{k})
.
That is to say,

(\bar{\pi}_{0,k})_{\ast}\omega_{D}(\sigma_{0};\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{k})=W_{D}(\sigma_{0};\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{k}).

Remark 5.3
.

In the case where
L=\mathcal{O}_{X}(D)
and
\sigma_{0}=1\in H^{0}(X,\mathcal{O}_{X})
, our construction of the logarithmic Wronskian is identified with the construction in
[
BD19
]
.

5.2.
Logarithmic Wronskian ideal sheaf

Let
X
be a projective manifold, and take an effective divisor
D
on
X
.
For a linear system
\mathfrak{S}\subset|L|
on
X
, we define

\mathfrak{S}_{\setminus D}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\{D^{\prime}\in|L\otimes\mathcal{O}_{X}(-D)|\mid D+D^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{S}\}.

Take a logarithmic directed projective manifold
(X,D,V(-\log D))
, and choose a linear system
\mathfrak{S}\subset|L|
on
X
such that
\mathfrak{S}_{\setminus D}
is non-empty.
Set

\mathbb{W}(X_{k}(D),\mathfrak{S})\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\Span\{\omega_{D}(\sigma_{0};\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{k})\mid\sigma_{0}\in\mathbb{S}_{\setminus D},\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{k}\in\mathbb{S}\},

which is a linear subspace of
H^{0}(X_{k}(D),\mathcal{O}_{X_{k}(D)}(k^{\prime})\otimes\bar{\pi}_{0,k}^{\ast}(L^{k+1}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{X}(-D)))
,
where
\mathbb{S}_{\setminus D}\subset H^{0}(X,L\otimes\mathcal{O}_{X}(-D))
is the linear subspace corresponding to
\mathfrak{S}_{\setminus D}
.
We thus denote by
\mathfrak{W}(X_{k}(D),\mathfrak{S})
the linear system corresponding to
\mathbb{W}(X_{k}(D),\mathfrak{S})
.
And we refer to its base ideal
\mathfrak{w}(X_{k}(D),\mathfrak{S})
as the
k
-th logarithmic Wronskian ideal sheaf of
\mathfrak{S}
.

Thanks to Proposition
5.2
, given a linear system
\mathfrak{S}\subset|L|
as above, there is a inclusion

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\bar{\pi}_{0,k})_{\ast}\mathbb{W}(X_{k}(D),\mathfrak{S})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}(X,E_{k,k^{\prime}}V^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}\!\otimes L^{k+1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}(X_{k},\mathcal{O}_{X_{k}}(k^{\prime})\otimes\pi_{0,k}^{\ast}L^{k+1}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{D}\cdot}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

Then we denote its image by

\mathbb{W}(X_{k},D,\mathfrak{S})=\Span\{\omega(\sigma_{0},\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{k})\mid\sigma_{0}\in\mathbb{S}_{\supset D},\,\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{k}\in\mathbb{S}\},

where
\mathbb{S}_{\supset D}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\sigma_{D}\cdot\mathbb{S}_{\setminus D}=\{\sigma\in\mathbb{S}\mid(\sigma)\geqslant D\}\cup\{0\}
.
Hence we denote the corresponding linear system by
\mathfrak{W}(X_{k},D,\mathfrak{S})
, and denote the base ideal of
\mathfrak{W}(X_{k},D,\mathfrak{S})
by
\mathfrak{w}(X_{k},D,\mathfrak{S})
.

More generally, for a closed subscheme
Z
of
X
, take a linear system
\mathfrak{S}\subset|L|
on
X
such that
\mathfrak{S}_{\supset Z}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\{D^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{S}\mid Z\subset D^{\prime}\}
is non-empty.
Then we introduce the transparent notation for a linear subspace of
H^{0}(X_{k},\mathcal{O}_{X_{k}}(k^{\prime})\otimes\pi_{0,k}^{\ast}L^{k+1})

\mathbb{W}(X_{k},Z,\mathfrak{S})\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\Span\{\omega(\sigma_{0},\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{k})\mid\sigma_{0}\in\mathbb{S}_{\supset Z},\,\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{k}\in\mathbb{S}\},

where
\mathbb{S}_{\supset Z}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\{\sigma\in\mathbb{S}\mid Z\subset(\sigma)\}
is the linear subspace corresponding to
\mathfrak{S}_{\supset Z}
.
We denote the corresponding linear system by
\mathfrak{W}(X_{k},Z,\mathfrak{S})
.
Therefore, we call its base ideal
\mathfrak{w}(X_{k},Z,\mathfrak{S})
the
k
-th Wronskian ideal sheaf of
\mathfrak{S}
along
Z
.

Moreover, set

(5.2)

\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}(X_{k},Z,\mathfrak{S})\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\Span\{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}(\sigma_{0},\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{k})\mid\sigma_{0}\in\mathbb{S}_{\supset Z},\,\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{k}\in\mathbb{S}\}.

Likewise, we denote the corresponding linear system by
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}(X_{k},Z,\mathfrak{S})
, and denote the base ideal of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}(X_{k},Z,\mathfrak{S})
by
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}(X_{k},Z,\mathfrak{S})
.

6.
Preliminaries of Nevanlinna theory

6.1.
Weil functions

First, we recall Weil functions
\lambda_{Z}
for each closed subscheme
Z
of a (complex) irreducible projective variety
X
, which is first introduced by Silverman
[
Sil87
]
in Diophantine geometry.

Given a (complex) irreducible quasi-projective variety
X
, let
\mathcal{C}(X)
be the set of all real-valued continuous functions defined on some Zariski open subsets of
X
,
that is to say,

\mathcal{C}(X)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\{\lambda\colon U\to\mathbb{R}\mid\lambda\text{ is continuous, }U\text{ is a Zariski open subset of }X\}.

Definition 6.1
.

Let
X
be as above, and let
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\in\mathcal{C}(X)
.
We write
\lambda_{1}\leqslant_{\langle X\rangle}\lambda_{2}
if there is a continuous function
\alpha
defined on
X
and satisfies that
\lambda_{1}\leqslant\lambda_{2}+\alpha
on some non-empty Zariski open subset
U
where
\lambda_{1}
and
\lambda_{2}
are both defined.
Moreover, we write
\lambda_{1}=_{\langle X\rangle}\lambda_{2}
, if
\lambda_{1}\leqslant_{\langle X\rangle}\lambda_{2}
and
\lambda_{2}\leqslant_{\langle X\rangle}\lambda_{1}
.

Notice that
=_{\langle X\rangle}
defines the equivalent relation on the set
\mathcal{C}(X)
, and
\leqslant_{\langle X\rangle}
defines the order relation on the set
\mathcal{C}(X)/=_{\langle X\rangle}
.
Furthermore, if
\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2},\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\in\mathcal{C}(X)
such that
\kappa_{1}=_{\langle X\rangle}\kappa_{2}
and
\lambda_{1}=_{\langle X\rangle}\lambda_{2}
, we check that

\displaystyle-\lambda_{1}

\displaystyle=_{\langle X\rangle}-\lambda_{2},

\displaystyle\kappa_{1}+\lambda_{1}

\displaystyle=_{\langle X\rangle}\kappa_{2}+\lambda_{2},

\displaystyle\min\{\kappa_{1},\lambda_{1}\}

\displaystyle=_{\langle X\rangle}\min\{\kappa_{2},\lambda_{2}\},

\displaystyle\max\{\kappa_{1},\lambda_{1}\}

\displaystyle=_{\langle X\rangle}\max\{\kappa_{1},\lambda_{1}\}.

Definition 6.2
.

Let
Z\subset X
be a closed subscheme. A Weil function
\lambda_{Z}
for
Z
is a continuous function
\lambda_{Z}:X\setminus\Supp Z\to\mathbb{R}
satisfying that there exist an affine Zariski open covering
\{U_{\epsilon}\}_{\epsilon\in\Upsilon}
of
X
and a system of generators
f_{1}^{\epsilon},\ldots,f_{r_{\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}\in\mathcal{I}_{Z}(U_{\epsilon})\subset\mathcal{O}_{X}(U_{\epsilon})
such that

(6.1)

\lambda_{Z}(x)=_{\langle U_{\epsilon}\rangle}-\log\mathop{\max}_{1\leqslant i\leqslant r_{\epsilon}}|f_{i}^{\epsilon}(x)|.

Remark 6.3
.

Observe the fact that
\infty
-norm is equivalent to
p
-norm, for each integer
p\geqslant 1
.
More precisely,

\log\mathop{\max}_{1\leqslant i\leqslant r}|\alpha_{i}|=_{\langle U\rangle}\frac{1}{p}\log\left(\sum_{i=1}^{r}|\alpha_{i}|^{p}\right),

for any Zariski open subset
U\subset X
.
Thus (
6.1
) can be also written as

\lambda_{Z}(x)=_{\langle U_{\epsilon}\rangle}-\frac{1}{p}\log\left(\sum_{i=1}^{r_{\epsilon}}|f_{i}^{\epsilon}(x)|^{p}\right).

Let
Z(X)
denote the set of closed subschemes of
X
.
Indeed, the definition above gives a map from
Z(X)
to
\mathcal{C}(X)/=_{\langle X\rangle}

\lambda\colon Z(X)\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}(X)/=_{\langle X\rangle}.

Notice that closed subschemes
Z\in Z(X)
are in one-to-one correspondence with ideal sheaves
\mathcal{I}_{Z}\subset\mathcal{O}_{X}
(see Chapter II, Section 5.9 of
[
Har77
]
).
We will often implicitly identify a closed subscheme
Z
with its ideal sheaf
\mathcal{I}_{Z}
.

Next, we need to recall the definitions of a number of natural operations on
Z(X)
, before going to introduce some properties of Weil functions
\lambda_{Z}
.

Definition 6.4
.

Let
Z_{1},Z_{2}
be closed subschemes of
X
.

i)

The intersection
Z_{1}\cap Z_{2}
of
Z_{1}
and
Z_{2}
is the closed subscheme of
X
with the ideal sheaf
\mathcal{I}_{Z_{1}\cap Z_{2}}=\mathcal{I}_{Z_{1}}+\mathcal{I}_{Z_{2}}
.

ii)

The sum
Z_{1}+Z_{2}
of
Z_{1}
and
Z_{2}
is the closed subscheme of
X
with the ideal sheaf
\mathcal{I}_{Z_{1}+Z_{2}}=\mathcal{I}_{Z_{1}}\cdot\mathcal{I}_{Z_{2}}
.

iii)

We say that
Z_{1}
is contained in
Z_{2}
, i.e.,
Z_{1}\subset Z_{2}
, if and only if
\mathcal{I}_{Z_{2}}\subset\mathcal{I}_{Z_{1}}
.

iv)

Let
\varphi\colon X\to Y
be a morphism of varieties, and let
Z
be a closed subscheme of
Y
. The inverse image
\varphi^{\ast}Z
is the closed subscheme of
X
with the ideal sheaf
\mathcal{I}_{\varphi^{\ast}Z}=\varphi^{-1}\mathcal{I}_{Z}\cdot\mathcal{O}_{X}
, where
\varphi^{-1}\mathcal{I}_{Z}\cdot\mathcal{O}_{X}
is the image in
\mathcal{O}_{X}
of the sheaf
\varphi^{\ast}\mathcal{I}_{Z}\subset\varphi^{\ast}\mathcal{O}_{Y}
.

Hence the map
\lambda
satisfies the following properties.

Proposition 6.5
.

[
Yam04
]

Let
Z_{1},Z_{2}
be closed subschemes of
X
. Then

i)

\lambda_{Z_{1}\cap Z_{2}}=_{\langle X\rangle}\min\{\lambda_{Z_{1}},\lambda_{Z_{2}}\}
.

ii)

\lambda_{Z_{1}+Z_{2}}=_{\langle X\rangle}\lambda_{Z_{1}}+\lambda_{Z_{2}}
.

iii)

If
Z_{1}\subset Z_{2}
, then
\lambda_{Z_{1}}\leqslant_{\langle X\rangle}\lambda_{Z_{2}}
.

iv)

If
\Supp Z_{1}\subset\Supp Z_{2}
, then there exists a constant
c>0
such that
\lambda_{Z_{1}}\leqslant_{\langle X\rangle}c\lambda_{Z_{2}}
.

v)

If
\varphi\colon X\to Y
is a morphism of varieties, then we have
\lambda_{\varphi^{\ast}Z}=_{\langle X\rangle}\lambda_{Z}\circ\varphi
for a closed subscheme
Z
of
Y
.

What’s more, think of an effective Cartier divisor
D
on
X
as a locally principal closed subscheme of
X
.
Assume that
X
as above is non-singular besides, and let
\sigma
be a canonical section of
\mathcal{O}(D)
, namely,
(\sigma)=D
.
Choose a continuous Hermitian metric
h
on the corresponding line bundle
\mathcal{O}(D)
with the weight function
\varphi
.
Take an affine Zariski open subset
U\subset X
such that
\mathcal{O}(D)_{\restriction U}
can be trivialized.
Thanks to the continuity of
\varphi
on
U
, if
\sigma_{U}
is associated to
\sigma
under a trivialization, then

\log|\sigma_{U}|=_{\langle U\rangle}\log\|\sigma\|_{h}.

Obviously, all such open subset
U
give an affine Zariski open covering of
X
.
Therefore, a Weil function
\lambda_{D}
for
D
can be also defined as a real-valued continuous function on
X\setminus\Supp D
satisfying

(6.2)

\lambda_{D}=_{\langle X\rangle}-\log\|\sigma\|_{h}.

Remark 6.6
.

More generally, if
D
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier divisor and
\ell D
is an effective Cartier divisor, then we can define
\lambda_{D}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\dfrac{1}{\ell}\lambda_{\ell D}
.
This definition is well-defined on account of Proposition
6.5
,
ii
).

6.2.
Notation and Terminology in Nevanlinna theory

From now on, we are going to prepare the notation and some well known results in Nevanlinna theory.

On a complex manifold
X
, the exterior derivative
\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}
takes a complex function and yields a complex
1
-form.
It decomposes as
\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}=\partial+\bar{\partial}
as complex
1
-forms decompose into
(1,0)
-forms and
(0,1)
-forms.
More concretely, take an open subset
U\subset X
with a local coordinate system
z_{1},\dots,z_{n}
, and we will write

\partial f=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{\partial f}{\partial z_{j}}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}z_{j},\qquad\bar{\partial}f=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{\partial f}{\partial\bar{z}_{j}}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\bar{z}_{j},

for a complex function
f
on
U
.
These operators can be automatically extended to forms of higher degree (see Chapter 0, Section 2.3 of
[
GH78
]
for details).
By convention, set
\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}^{c}=\dfrac{i}{4\pi}(\bar{\partial}-\partial)
.
We have
\mathop{}\!\textnormal{dd}^{c}=\dfrac{i}{2\pi}\partial\bar{\partial}
.

Let us recall the singular Hermitian metric and the curvature current of a holomorphic line bundle.

Definition 6.7
.

[
Dem92
]

Let
L\to X
be a holomorphic line bundle over a complex manifold
X
.
We say that
h
is a singular metric on
L
if for any local trivialization
L_{\restriction U}\cong U\times\mathbb{C}
of
L
, the metric is given by

(6.3)

\|\xi\|_{h}^{2}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=|\xi|^{2}e^{-\varphi}

where
\varphi\in L^{1}_{\loc}(U)
is a real valued function, called the weight function of the metric
h
with respect to the such local trivialization.
We say that
h
admits a closed subset
\Sing(h)\subset X
as its degeneration set if
\varphi
is locally bounded on
X\setminus\Sing(h)
and is unbounded on a neighborhood of any point of
\Sing(h)
.

If
\varphi^{\prime}
is the weight function of the metric
h
with respect to another local trivialization, then we see that
\varphi^{\prime}=\varphi+\log|g|
where
g
is the transition function between two local trivializations.
The curvature current of
L
is then defined to be the closed
(1,1)
-current
\mathop{}\!\textnormal{dd}^{c}[\varphi]
, which is independent of choice of local trivializations.
And the assumption
\varphi\in L^{1}_{\loc}
guarantees that
\mathop{}\!\textnormal{dd}^{c}[\varphi]
exists in the sense of distributions.

We will always assume here that the weight function
\varphi
is quasi plurisubharmonic.
Recall that a function
\varphi
is said to be quasi plurisubharmonic if
\varphi
is locally the sum of a plurisubharmonic function and of a smooth function (so that in particular
\varphi\in L^{1}_{\loc}
).
Then the curvature current
\mathop{}\!\textnormal{dd}^{c}[\varphi]
is well-defined as a current and is locally bounded from below by a negative
(1,1)
-form with constant coefficients.
In addition,
\mathop{}\!\textnormal{dd}^{c}[\varphi]
is in the first Chern class
c_{1}(L,h)
.

Example 6.8
.

Take a linear system
\mathfrak{S}\subset|L|
on a projective complex manifold
X
.
One can define a natural (possibly singular) Hermitian metric
h_{\mathfrak{S}}
on
L
, whose weight function
\varphi_{\mathfrak{S}}
is given by

(6.4)

\varphi_{\mathfrak{S}}=\log\left(\sum_{i=0}^{N}|\varsigma_{i}|^{2}\right),

where
N=\dim\mathfrak{S}
,
\{\varsigma_{i}\mid i=0,1,\dots,N\}
is a basis of
\mathbb{S}
, and
\mathbb{S}\subset H^{0}(X,L)
is the linear subspace corresponding to
\mathfrak{S}
.
Obviously,
\Sing(h_{\mathfrak{S}})=\Bs(\mathfrak{S})
.
Accordingly, the metric
h_{\mathfrak{S}}
is smooth if and only if
\mathfrak{S}
is base point free.

Observe that
|\sigma|^{2}=e^{\varphi_{\mathfrak{S}}}
in the particular case where
\mathfrak{S}=\{D\}\subset|D|
.
Taking logarithms on both sides of (
6.3
), we immediately get

-\log\|\sigma_{D}\|_{h}=\frac{1}{2}(\varphi-\varphi_{\mathfrak{S}}),

for any (possibly singular) Hermitian metric
h
on
\mathcal{O}(D)
with the weighted function
\varphi
.
And it gives a Weil function for
D
on account of (
6.2
), if
h
is a continuous Hermitian metric.

More generally, take a linear system
\mathfrak{S}\subset|L|
on a projective complex manifold
X
.
Let us introduce the transparent notation
Z_{\mathfrak{s}}
for the closed subscheme of
X
with the ideal sheaf
\mathcal{I}_{Z_{\mathfrak{s}}}=\mathfrak{s}
, where
\mathfrak{s}
is the base ideal of
\mathfrak{S}
.
We say that
Z_{\mathfrak{s}}
is the scheme of base points of
\mathfrak{S}
.
Take a basis
\{\varsigma_{i}\mid i=0,1,\dots,N\}
of
\mathbb{S}
, where
N=\dim\mathfrak{S}
,
\mathbb{S}\subset H^{0}(X,L)
is the linear subspace corresponding to
\mathfrak{S}
.
It is evident that

Z_{\mathfrak{s}}=\bigcap_{i=0}^{N}D_{i},

where
D_{i}=(\varsigma_{i})
for each
0\leqslant i\leqslant N
.
According to Proposition
6.5
,
i
) and Remark
6.3
,

(6.5)

\lambda_{Z_{\mathfrak{s}}}=_{\langle X\rangle}-\log\mathop{\max}_{0\leqslant i\leqslant N}\|\varsigma_{i}\|_{h}=_{\langle X\rangle}\frac{1}{2}(\varphi-\varphi_{\mathfrak{S}}),

where
\varphi
is the weighted function of a continuous Hermitian metric
h
on
L
.

Definition 6.9
.

Let
\Phi\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime(1,1)}(\mathbb{C})
be a current of bidegree
(1,1)
(equivalently, of bidimension
(0,0)
) such that
\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\Phi=0
and
\Phi
is representable by integration. One defines

\mathcal{N}(\Phi,r)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\Phi\left(\log^{+}\!\frac{r}{|z|}\right)=\int_{0}^{r}\frac{\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}t}{t}\int_{\bigtriangleup(t)}\!\Phi,

where
\log^{+}\!t\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\max\{\log t,0\}
for any
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
.

Now, let
f\colon\mathbb{C}\to X
be a holomorphic curve in a projective manifold
X
.
Let
Z\subset X
be a closed subscheme such that
f(\mathbb{C})\not\subset\Supp Z
.
Choose a Weil function
\lambda_{Z}
for
Z
.
Thereupon one defines the proximity function of
f
for
Z
by

m_{f}(r,Z)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\int_{\partial\bigtriangleup(r)}\!\lambda_{Z}\circ f\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}^{c}|z|^{2}=\int_{0}^{2\pi}\!\lambda_{Z}\circ f(re^{i\theta})\frac{\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\theta}{2\pi}.

According to Proposition
6.5
,
iii
), we have
0\leqslant_{\langle X\rangle}\lambda_{Z}
.
By definition, there is a continuous function
\alpha
defined on
X
and satisfies that
0\leqslant\lambda_{Z}+\alpha
on some non-empty Zariski open subset.
Thanks to the compactness of
X
,
\alpha
is indeed bounded on
X
.
Consequently,
0\leqslant m_{f}(r,Z)+O(1)
.
Here
O(\bullet)
is the Landau symbol “big-O”.
Consider a non-negative real-valued function
\varphi\colon\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}
.

O(\varphi)
represents any quantity
\psi\colon\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfying that

|\psi(r)|\leqslant c\,\varphi(r)

for some constant
c>0
and all
r\in\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}
.
In particular,
O(1)
denotes all bounded real-valued functions on
\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}
.
If choosing another Weil function
\lambda^{\prime}_{Z}
for
Z
, we obtain a new function
m^{\prime}_{f}(r,Z)
.
Likewise, we see that
m_{f}(r,Z)=m^{\prime}_{f}(r,Z)+O(1)
.
Hence we prefer to consider the proximity functions as functions modulo
O(1)
.

Next, let
f
and
Z
be as above. One also defines the counting function of
f
for
Z
by

N_{f}(r,Z)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\mathcal{N}(f^{\ast}[Z],r).

Finally, one defines the characteristic function (or order function) of
f
for
Z
by

T_{f}(r,Z)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=m_{f}(r,Z)+N_{f}(r,Z).

6.3.
First main theorem

Now, investigate the particular case where
Z
is a (Cartier) divisor
D
.
From (
6.2
), it allows us to choose the Weil function
\lambda_{D}=-\log\|\sigma\|_{h}
for some continuous Hermitian metric
h
on
\mathcal{O}(D)
.
We thus write

m_{f}(r,D)=-\int_{0}^{2\pi}\!\log\|\sigma\circ f(re^{i\theta})\|_{h}\frac{\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\theta}{2\pi},

where
\sigma
be a canonical section of
\mathcal{O}(D)
.
Let us recall the Poincaré-Lelong formula (cf.
[
Lel64
]
) and the Green-Jensen formula, which play important roles in Nevanlinna theory.

Theorem 6.10

(Poincaré-Lelong formula)
.

Let
f\not\equiv 0
be a meromorphic function on a complex manifold
X
of dimension
n
.
Let
\eta
be a
(2n-2)
-form of
C^{2}
-class on
X
with compact support. Then

\int_{X}\log|f|^{2}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{dd}^{c}\eta=\int_{D_{f}}\!\eta,

which amounts to saying that, in the sense of currents,

\mathop{}\!\textnormal{dd}^{c}[\log|f|^{2}]=[D_{f}],

where
D_{f}
is a divisor locally expressed by
(f_{1})-(f_{2})
, if
f
is locally expressed by the ratio
f=\dfrac{f_{1}}{f_{2}}
with
f_{2}\not\equiv 0
.

Proof.

See Theorem 5.1.13 in
[
NO90
]
or Theorem 2.2.16 in
[
NW14
]
.
∎

Moreover, we also have the following theorem as an application of the Poincaré-Lelong formula to global sections.

Theorem 6.11
.

Let
X
be a complex manifold, let
D
be an effective divisor on
X
with
D=(\sigma)
. Choose a continuous Hermitian metric
h
on
\mathcal{O}(D)
with the weight function
\varphi
. Then
\log\|\sigma\|_{h}^{2}\in L^{1}_{\loc}
and

\mathop{}\!\textnormal{dd}^{c}[\log\|\sigma\|_{h}^{2}]=[D]-[\mathop{}\!\textnormal{dd}^{c}\varphi].

Proof.

See Lemma 1.12 in
[
CG72
]
.
∎

Theorem 6.12

(Green-Jensen formula)
.

Let
g\colon\mathbb{C}\to[-\infty,\infty]
be a function on
\mathbb{C}
such that
\mathop{}\!\textnormal{dd}^{c}[g]
is of order zero, that is to say,
g
can be locally written as a difference of two subharmonic functions.
Suppose that
g(0)
is finite. Then

\mathcal{N}(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{dd}^{c}[g],r)=\frac{1}{2}\left(\int_{0}^{2\pi}\!g(re^{i\theta})\frac{\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\theta}{2\pi}-g(0)\right).

Proof.

Actually, this theorem is just the one variable case of Corollary 2.1.37 in
[
NW14
]
with the supposition that the Lelong number of
\mathop{}\!\textnormal{dd}^{c}[g]
at the origin
0
is zero.
∎

Suppose
f(0)\notin D
. It follows from the Green-Jensen formula and Theorem
6.11
that

\displaystyle m_{f}(r,D)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\!\log\|\sigma\circ f(re^{i\theta})\|_{h}^{2}\frac{\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\theta}{2\pi}

\displaystyle=-\mathcal{N}(f^{\ast}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{dd}^{c}[\log\|\sigma\|_{h}^{2}],r)-\log\|\sigma\circ f(0)\|_{h}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{N}(f^{\ast}[\mathop{}\!\textnormal{dd}^{c}\varphi],r)-\mathcal{N}(f^{\ast}[D],r)-\log\|\sigma\circ f(0)\|_{h}.

Therefore,

(6.6)

T_{f}(r,D)=\mathcal{N}(f^{\ast}[\mathop{}\!\textnormal{dd}^{c}\varphi],r)-\log\|\sigma\circ f(0)\|_{h}.

This implies that as a function modulo
O(1)
,
T_{f}(r,D)
merely depends on the line bundle
\mathcal{O}(D)
associated to the divisor
D
.
Accordingly, for any holomorphic line bundle
L
, one can define the characteristic function modulo
O(1)
of
f
for
L
by

T_{f}(r,L)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\mathcal{N}(f^{\ast}[\mathop{}\!\textnormal{dd}^{c}\varphi],r),

where
\varphi
is the weight function of some continuous Hermitian metric
h
on
L
.
If choosing another continuous Hermitian metric
h^{\prime}
on
L
with the weight function
\varphi^{\prime}
we obtain a new function
T^{\prime}_{f}(r,L)
.
Thanks to the compactness of
X
,
\varphi-\varphi^{\prime}
is a bounded function on
X
.
By the Green-Jensen formula,

T_{f}(r,L)-T^{\prime}_{f}(r,L)=\frac{1}{2}\left(\int_{0}^{2\pi}\!(\varphi(re^{i\theta})-\varphi^{\prime}(re^{i\theta}))\frac{\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\theta}{2\pi}-\varphi(0)-\varphi^{\prime}(0)\right)=O(1).

Consequently,
T_{f}(r,L)
is well-defined as a function modulo
O(1)
.

Remark 6.13
.

Actually, the discussion above is still valid for the case where
f(0)\in D
.
If
f(0)\in D
, then we can write
\|\sigma\circ f(z)\|_{h}^{2}=|z|^{2m}g^{2}(z)
with
g(0)\neq 0
, where
\sigma\circ f
has a zero of order
m
at the origin
0
.
Actually,
m
is the Lelong number of the current
\mathop{}\!\textnormal{dd}^{c}[\log\|\sigma\circ f\|_{h}^{2}]
at the origin
0
.
Hence

\displaystyle m_{f}(r,D)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\!\log g^{2}(re^{i\theta})\frac{\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\theta}{2\pi}-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\!\log|re^{i\theta}z|^{2m}\frac{\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\theta}{2\pi}

\displaystyle=-\mathcal{N}(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{dd}^{c}[\log g^{2}],r)-\mathcal{N}(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{dd}^{c}[\log|z|^{2m}],r)-\log|g(0)|

\displaystyle=-\mathcal{N}(f^{\ast}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{dd}^{c}[\log\|\sigma\|_{h}^{2}],r)-\log|g(0)|

\displaystyle=\mathcal{N}(f^{\ast}[\mathop{}\!\textnormal{dd}^{c}\varphi],r)-\mathcal{N}(f^{\ast}[D],r)-\log|g(0)|.

Furthermore, by (
6.6
), one easily concludes the first main theorem (FMT for short) in Nevanlinna Theory.

Theorem 6.14

(FMT)
.

Let
X
be a projective manifold, and let
L
be a (holomorphic) line bundle on
X
.
Take a Cartier divisor
D
on
X
such that
\mathcal{O}(D)=L
. Take two effective divisors
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
satisfying
D=D_{1}-D_{2}
, if necessary. Then

T_{f}(r,L)=T_{f}(r,D_{1})-T_{f}(r,D_{2})+O(1),

for any holomorphic curve
f\colon\mathbb{C}\to X
such that
f(\mathbb{C})\not\subset\Supp D
.

6.4.
Crofton’s formula

Let us consider the unitary group
\textnormal{U}_{n+1}
, which acts on the left on
\Gr(k,\mathbb{P}^{n})\cong\Gr(k+1,n+1)
, and especially on
\mathbb{P}^{n}\cong\Gr(1,n+1)
.
Technically, one can replace
\textnormal{U}_{n+1}
by the special unitary group
\textnormal{SU}_{n+1}
, which acts effectively on
\Gr(k,\mathbb{P}^{n})
and on
\mathbb{P}^{n}
.
Let
\mu
denote the Haar measure on
\textnormal{U}_{n+1}
normalized such that
\mu(\textnormal{U}_{n+1})=1
.

Shiffman
[
Shi74
]
established the following lemma as the central ingredient in his proof of Crofton’s formula (on complex manifolds) and its generalizations.
We call this lemma Crofton’s formula for currents on
\mathbb{P}^{n}
.

Lemma 6.15
.

Let
\Phi\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime(k,k)}(\mathbb{P}^{n})
be a current of bidegree
(k,k)
. For any
\eta\in\mathcal{D}^{(n-k,n-k)}(\mathbb{P}^{n})
,

\int_{\textnormal{U}_{n+1}}\hskip-10.00002pt\Phi(g^{\ast}\eta)\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\mu(g)=c\int_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}\!\omega^{k}\wedge\eta,

where
c=\Phi(\omega^{n-k})
and
\omega
is the Kähler form associated to the Fubini-Study metric on
\mathbb{P}^{n}
.
If
\Phi
is representable by integration, then the above formula holds for all
(n-k,n-k)
-form
\eta
of
C^{0}
-class on
\mathbb{P}^{n}
with compact support.
In other words,

\int_{\textnormal{U}_{n+1}}\hskip-10.00002ptg_{\ast}\Phi\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\mu(g)=c[\omega^{k}],

and it is valid for
C^{0}
forms, if
\Phi
is representable by integration.

Now, apply this lemma with
\Phi=[H]
, where
H
is a hyperplane of
\mathbb{P}^{n}
.
Notice that
[H](\omega^{n-1})=1
and the Fubini-Study metric on
\mathbb{P}^{n}
can be regarded as a Hermitian metric
h
on hyperplane line bundle
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(1)
.
Thanks to Theorem
6.11
, we obtain

\int_{\textnormal{U}_{n+1}}\hskip-10.00002ptg_{\ast}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{dd}^{c}[\log\|\sigma\|_{h}]\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\mu(g)=0,

where
\sigma
is a canonical section of
\mathcal{O}(H)
.

What’s more, the following lemma gives the average of Weil functions for all the hyperplanes of
\mathbb{P}^{n}
in the sense of
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{P}^{n})/=_{\langle\mathbb{P}^{n}\rangle}
.

Lemma 6.16
.

Let
H
be a hyperplane of
\mathbb{P}^{n}
. Take a fixed canonical section
\sigma
of
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(H)
. Then

\int_{\textnormal{U}_{n+1}}\hskip-10.00002ptg^{\ast}\log\|\sigma(x)\|_{h}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\mu(g)=c,

for every
x\in\mathbb{P}^{n}
, where
c
is a constant.
It implies that

(6.7)

\int_{\textnormal{U}_{n+1}}\hskip-10.00002ptg^{\ast}\lambda_{H}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\mu(g)=_{\langle\mathbb{P}^{n}\rangle}0.

Proof.

Seeing that the unitary group
\textnormal{U}_{n+1}
acts transitively on
\mathbb{P}^{n}
, the improper integral

\int_{\textnormal{U}_{n+1}}\hskip-10.00002ptg^{\ast}\log\|\sigma(x)\|_{h}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\mu(g)=\int_{\textnormal{U}_{n+1}}\hskip-10.00002pt\log\|\sigma(g\cdot x)\|_{h}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\mu(g)

equals to a constant
c
independent of
x\in\mathbb{P}^{n}
(cf. the reasoning in the proof of Proposition 3.5 in
[
CG76
]
).
∎

Remark 6.17
.

Think of the complete linear system
\mathfrak{S}=|H|
as the dual projective space
(\mathbb{P}^{n})^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}
.
Then
g^{\ast}H
defines a right action
g\in\textnormal{U}_{N+1}
on
H\in\mathfrak{S}
.
There is a unique measure
\rho
on
\mathfrak{S}\cong(\mathbb{P}^{n})^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}
, which is invariant under the unitary group
\textnormal{U}_{n+1}
and satisfies
\rho(\mathfrak{S})=1
.
Thus (
6.7
) can be reformulated as

\int_{H\in\mathfrak{S}}\hskip-10.00002pt\lambda_{H}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\rho(H)=_{\langle\mathbb{P}^{n}\rangle}0.

Take an arbitrary linear system
\mathfrak{S}\subset|L|
on a projective manifold
X
.
Observe that effective divisors
D\in\mathfrak{S}
are in one-to-one correspondence with hyperplanes
H_{D}
of
\mathfrak{S}^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}\cong\mathbb{P}^{n}
.
Therefore, the unitary group
\textnormal{U}_{N+1}
acts on the right on
\mathfrak{S}
, where
N=\dim\mathfrak{S}
.
More precisely, an action
g\in\textnormal{U}_{N+1}
on
D\in\mathfrak{S}
is described by
g^{\ast}D
, which corresponds to the hyperplane
g^{\ast}H_{D}
of
\mathfrak{S}^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}
.
Accordingly, we generalize Lemma
6.16
to divisors on projective manifolds.

Theorem 6.18
.

Let
X
be a projective manifold, and take a linear system
\mathfrak{S}\subset|L|
on
X
.
Given a divisor
D\in\mathfrak{S}
, we have

(6.8)

\int_{\textnormal{U}_{N+1}}\hskip-10.00002ptg^{\ast}\lambda_{D}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\mu(g)=_{\langle X\rangle}\lambda_{Z_{\mathfrak{s}}},

where we hereinafter write, by abuse of notation,
g^{\ast}\lambda_{D}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\lambda_{g^{\ast}D}
, and
\mu
is the Haar measure on
\textnormal{U}_{N+1}
normalized such that
\mu(\textnormal{U}_{N+1})=1
.

Proof.

Let
\mathbb{S}\subset H^{0}(X,L)
be the linear subspace corresponding to
\mathfrak{S}
.
Take a basis
\{\varsigma_{i}\mid i=0,1,\dots,N\}
of
\mathbb{S}
, where
N=\dim\mathfrak{S}
.
We naturally have a rational map

(6.9)

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathfrak{S}}\colon X

\displaystyle\dashrightarrow\mathfrak{S}^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}\cong\mathbb{P}^{N}

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\longmapsto[\varsigma_{0}(x):\dots:\varsigma_{N}(x)].

Then we are going to discuss the linear system
\mathfrak{S}
into three different cases.

If
\mathfrak{S}
is base point free, then
\mu_{\mathfrak{S}}
is holomorphic.
We see that
D=\mu_{\mathfrak{S}}^{\ast}H_{D}
, for each
D\in\mathfrak{S}
.
In this case, the theorem immediately follows from Proposition
6.5
,
v
) and Lemma
6.16
.

Next, investigate the case when
\mathfrak{S}
is not base point free and its base ideal
\mathfrak{s}=\mathcal{O}_{X}(-D_{0})
for an effective Cartier divisor
D_{0}
, i.e.,
Z_{\mathfrak{s}}=D_{0}
.
Since
\mathfrak{S}_{\setminus D_{0}}
is base point free,
\mu_{\mathfrak{S}_{\setminus D_{0}}}
is a holomorphic map from
X
to
\mathfrak{S}_{\setminus D_{0}}^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}\cong\mathbb{P}^{N}
.
Regarding
\mathfrak{S}^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}
and
\mathfrak{S}_{\setminus D_{0}}^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}
as the same projective space, we easily check that
\mu_{\mathfrak{S}_{\setminus D_{0}}\restriction U}=\mu_{\mathfrak{S}\restriction U}
, where
U=X\setminus\Supp D_{0}
.
It implies that
\mu_{\mathfrak{S}_{\setminus D_{0}}}
is actually the analytic extension of
\mu_{\mathfrak{S}}
.
Consequently,

\int_{\textnormal{U}_{N+1}}\hskip-10.00002ptg^{\ast}\lambda_{D}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\mu(g)=_{\langle X\rangle}\lambda_{D_{0}}+\int_{\textnormal{U}_{N+1}}\hskip-10.00002ptg^{\ast}\lambda_{D-D_{0}}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\mu(g)=_{\langle X\rangle}\lambda_{D_{0}}.

Finally, we just need to verify the case when
\mathfrak{S}
is not base point free and
Z_{\mathfrak{s}}
is not an effective Cartier divisor.
Take the blow-up of
X
with respect to
\mathfrak{s}

\upsilon\colon\widehat{X}\longrightarrow X,

and denote by
E_{0}=\upsilon^{\ast}Z_{\mathfrak{s}}
the exceptional divisor of
\upsilon
.
Hence there is a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{S}^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\upsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\mu}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}

Let us define
\upsilon^{\ast}\mathfrak{S}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\{\upsilon^{\ast}D\mid D\in\mathfrak{S}\}
.
Notice that
\upsilon^{\ast}\mathfrak{S}
satisfies the second case in our discussion.
Following the same reasoning as above and Proposition
6.5
,
v
), we obtain

\int_{\textnormal{U}_{N+1}}\hskip-10.00002ptg^{\ast}\lambda_{D}\circ\upsilon\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\mu(g)=_{\langle\widehat{X}\rangle}\int_{\textnormal{U}_{N+1}}\hskip-10.00002ptg^{\ast}\lambda_{\upsilon^{\ast}D}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\mu(g)=_{\langle\widehat{X}\rangle}\lambda_{E_{0}}=_{\langle\widehat{X}\rangle}\lambda_{Z_{\mathfrak{s}}}\circ\upsilon,

which amounts to saying that there exists a constant
a>0
such that

\lambda_{Z_{\mathfrak{s}}}\circ\upsilon(\hat{x})-a\leqslant\int_{\textnormal{U}_{N+1}}\hskip-10.00002ptg^{\ast}\lambda_{D}\circ\upsilon(\hat{x})\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\mu(g)\leqslant\lambda_{Z_{\mathfrak{s}}}\circ\upsilon(\hat{x})+a,

for every
\hat{x}\in\widehat{X}\setminus E_{0}
.
Seeing that
\upsilon
restricted on
\widehat{X}\setminus E_{0}
is an isomorphism, we have

\lambda_{Z_{\mathfrak{s}}}(x)-a\leqslant\int_{\textnormal{U}_{N+1}}\hskip-10.00002ptg^{\ast}\lambda_{D}(x)\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\mu(g)\leqslant\lambda_{Z_{\mathfrak{s}}}(x)+a,

for every
x\in X\setminus\Supp Z_{\mathfrak{s}}
.
By definition,

\int_{\textnormal{U}_{N+1}}\hskip-10.00002ptg^{\ast}\lambda_{D}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\mu(g)=_{\langle X\rangle}\lambda_{Z_{\mathfrak{s}}}.\qed

Remark 6.19
.

Analogous to Remark
6.17
, (
6.8
) is reformulated as

\int_{D\in\mathfrak{S}}\hskip-10.00002pt\lambda_{D}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\rho(D)=_{\langle X\rangle}\lambda_{Z_{\mathfrak{s}}},

where
\rho
is (slightly abusively) the unique measure on
\mathfrak{S}\cong(\mathbb{P}^{N})^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}
invariant under the unitary group
\textnormal{U}_{N+1}
and satisfying
\rho(\mathfrak{S})=1
.

We refer to Theorem
6.18
as Crofton’s formula for Weil functions for divisors on projective manifolds.

Take a linear system
\mathfrak{T}\subset|L|
on
X
.
We say that a subset
\mathfrak{S}\subset\mathfrak{T}
is a linear subsystem of
\mathfrak{T}
on
X
, if it is a linear subspace for the projective space structure of
\mathfrak{T}
.
For each
0\leqslant k\leqslant N-1
, consider the Grassmannian
\Gr(k,\mathfrak{T})
, which consist of all linear subsystems
\mathfrak{S}
of
\mathfrak{T}
on
X
such that
\dim\mathfrak{S}=k
, where
N=\dim\mathfrak{T}
.
We thus conclude Crofton’s formula for Weil functions for closed subschemes of projective manifolds.

Theorem 6.20
.

Let
X
be a projective manifold, and take a linear system
\mathfrak{T}\subset|L|
on
X
.
Choose a linear subsystem
\mathfrak{S}
of
\mathfrak{T}
on
X
such that
\dim\mathfrak{S}=k
. Then

(6.10)

\int_{\textnormal{U}_{N+1}}\hskip-10.00002pt\lambda_{g^{\ast}Z_{\mathfrak{s}}}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\mu(g)=_{\langle X\rangle}\lambda_{Z_{\mathfrak{t}}}.

Proof.

For any
\mathfrak{S}\in\Gr(k,\mathfrak{T})
, taking a basis
\{\varsigma_{i}\mid i=0,1,\dots,k\}
of
\mathbb{S}
,
we have
Z_{\mathfrak{s}}=\bigcap_{i=0}^{k}D_{i}
, where
D_{i}=(\varsigma_{i})
for each
0\leqslant i\leqslant k
.
Think of
\Gr(k,\mathfrak{T})
as the dual of
\Gr(k,\mathfrak{T}^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}})\cong\Gr(k+1,N+1)
.
The unitary group
\textnormal{U}_{N+1}
acts on the right on
\Gr(k,\mathfrak{T})\cong\Gr(k+1,N+1)^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}
.
In addition, it induces a right action on the set of the scheme of base points of all the
\mathfrak{S}\in\Gr(k,\mathfrak{T})
,
where an action
g\in\textnormal{U}_{N+1}
on
Z_{\mathfrak{s}}
can be described by

g^{\ast}Z_{\mathfrak{s}}=\bigcap_{i=0}^{k}g^{\ast}D_{i},

regardless of the choice of the basis.
By Proposition
6.5
,
iii
) and Theorem
6.18
, we get

\displaystyle\int_{\textnormal{U}_{N+1}}\hskip-10.00002pt\lambda_{g^{\ast}Z_{\mathfrak{s}}}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\mu(g)

\displaystyle=_{\langle X\rangle}\int_{\textnormal{U}_{N+1}}\!\mathop{\min}_{0\leqslant i\leqslant\ell}\lambda_{g^{\ast}D_{i}}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\mu(g)

\displaystyle\leqslant_{\langle X\rangle}\mathop{\min}_{0\leqslant i\leqslant\ell}\int_{\textnormal{U}_{N+1}}\hskip-10.00002pt\lambda_{g^{\ast}D_{i}}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\mu(g)

\displaystyle\leqslant_{\langle X\rangle}\lambda_{Z_{\mathfrak{t}}}.

On the other hand, since
\mathfrak{s}\subset\mathfrak{t}
, namely,
Z_{\mathfrak{t}}\subset Z_{\mathfrak{s}}
for all
\mathfrak{S}\in\Gr(k,\mathfrak{T})
, then Proposition
6.5
,
iii
) leads to

\lambda_{Z_{\mathfrak{t}}}\leqslant_{\langle X\rangle}\int_{\textnormal{U}_{N+1}}\hskip-10.00002pt\lambda_{g^{\ast}Z_{\mathfrak{s}}}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\mu(g).

Then the theorem is verified.
∎

Remark 6.21
.

Let
\rho_{k,N}
be the unique measure on
\Gr(k,\mathfrak{T})\cong\Gr(k+1,N+1)^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}
invariant under the unitary group
\textnormal{U}_{N+1}
and satisfying
\rho_{k,N}(\Gr(k,\mathfrak{T}))=1
.
Therefore, (
6.10
) is reformulated as

\int_{\mathfrak{S}\in\Gr(k,\mathfrak{T})}\hskip-10.00002pt\lambda_{Z_{\mathfrak{s}}}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\rho_{k,N}(\mathfrak{S})=_{\langle X\rangle}\lambda_{Z_{\mathfrak{t}}}.

In the particular case when
k=0
, we see that
\mathfrak{S}\in\Gr(0,\mathfrak{T})
corresponds to
D\in\mathfrak{T}
, which implies that Theorem
6.20
for
k=0
coincides with Theorem
6.18
.

Choose linear subsystems
\mathfrak{S}_{0},\dots,\mathfrak{S}_{\ell}
of
\mathfrak{T}\subset|L|
on
X
such that
\mathbb{T}=\mathbb{S}_{0}+\dots+\mathbb{S}_{\ell}
,
where
\mathbb{T}
,
\mathbb{S}_{0},\dots,\mathbb{S}_{\ell}
are the linear subspace of
H^{0}(X,L)
corresponding to
\mathfrak{T}
,
\mathfrak{S}_{0},\dots,\mathfrak{S}_{\ell}
, respectively.
Moreover, take another linear system
\mathfrak{S}
(not necessary lying in
|L|
) on
X
such that
\dim\mathfrak{S}\leqslant\dim\mathfrak{T}
, but
\dim\mathfrak{S}\geqslant\dim\mathfrak{S}_{i}
for each
0\leqslant i\leqslant\ell
.
Obviously, for each
0\leqslant i\leqslant\ell
, there exists a surjective homomorphism
\beta_{i}\colon\mathbb{S}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{S}_{i}
,
where
\mathbb{S}
are the linear space corresponding to
\mathfrak{S}
.
Take account of the following map

(6.11)

\displaystyle Z_{\beta}\colon\mathfrak{S}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow Z(X)

\displaystyle D

\displaystyle\longmapsto Z_{\beta}(D)=\bigcap_{i=0}^{\ell}\beta_{i}(D),

where
\beta_{i}(D)=(\beta_{i}(\sigma))
for a canonical section
\sigma
of
\mathcal{O}_{X}(D)
, and in particular,
\beta_{i}(D)=X
if
\beta_{i}(\sigma)=0
.
We make a convention that
\lambda_{X}\equiv\infty
as a generalized function.
Then we obtain a generalization of Crofton’s formula as follows.

Theorem 6.22
.

Let
X
be a projective manifold, and choose linear systems
\mathfrak{T}
,
\mathfrak{S}_{0},\dots,\mathfrak{S}_{\ell}
, and
\mathfrak{S}
on
X
as above.
Let
\beta_{i}
be as above, for each
0\leqslant i\leqslant\ell
.
Given a divisor
D\in\mathfrak{S}
, we have

(6.12)

\int_{\textnormal{U}_{N+1}}\hskip-10.00002pt\lambda_{Z_{\beta}(g^{\ast}D)}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\mu(g)=_{\langle X\rangle}\lambda_{Z_{\mathfrak{t}}},

where
N=\dim\mathfrak{S}
.

Proof.

For each
0\leqslant i\leqslant\ell
, the dual homomorphism
\beta_{i}^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}\colon\mathbb{S}_{i}^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{S}^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}
induces a closed embedding
\iota_{\beta_{i}^{\vee}}\colon\mathfrak{S}_{i}^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}\cong\textnormal{P}(\mathbb{S}_{i}^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}})\hookrightarrow\textnormal{P}(\mathbb{S}^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}})\cong\mathfrak{S}^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}
.
We easily check that
\beta_{i}(D)
corresponds to the hyperplane
\iota_{\beta_{i}^{\vee}}^{\ast}H_{D}
of
\mathfrak{S}_{i}^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}
if
\iota_{\beta_{i}^{\vee}}(\mathfrak{S}_{i}^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}})\not\subset\Supp H_{D}
, and
\beta_{i}(D)=X
, which corresponds to
\mathfrak{S}_{i}^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}
, if
\iota_{\beta_{i}^{\vee}}(\mathfrak{S}_{i}^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}})\subset\Supp H_{D}
.
By abuse of notation, let
\beta_{i}(D)
also denote the corresponding hyperplane of
\mathfrak{S}_{i}^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}
(or
\mathfrak{S}_{i}^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}
itself).
According to Proposition
6.5
,
v
), we get

\int_{\textnormal{U}_{N+1}}\hskip-10.00002pt\lambda_{\beta_{i}(g^{\ast}D)}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\mu(g)=_{\langle\mathfrak{S}_{i}^{\vee}\rangle}\int_{\textnormal{U}_{N+1}}\hskip-10.00002ptg^{\ast}\lambda_{H_{D}}\circ\iota_{\beta_{i}^{\vee}}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\mu(g)=_{\langle\mathfrak{S}_{i}^{\vee}\rangle}0.

Following the same reasoning in the proof of Theorem
6.18
, the above formula yields

(6.13)

\int_{\textnormal{U}_{N+1}}\hskip-10.00002pt\lambda_{\beta_{i}(g^{\ast}D)}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\mu(g)=_{\langle X\rangle}\lambda_{Z_{i}},

where
Z_{i}=Z_{\mathfrak{s}_{i}}
for each
0\leqslant i\leqslant\ell
.
We assert that the theorem follows from the same reasoning in the proof of Theorem
6.20
.
Concretely speaking, according to Proposition
6.5
,
i
) and (
6.13
),

\displaystyle\int_{\textnormal{U}_{N+1}}\hskip-10.00002pt\lambda_{Z_{\beta}(g^{\ast}D)}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\mu(g)

\displaystyle=_{\langle X\rangle}\int_{\textnormal{U}_{N+1}}\!\mathop{\min}_{0\leqslant i\leqslant\ell}\lambda_{\beta_{i}(g^{\ast}D)}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\mu(g)

\displaystyle\leqslant_{\langle X\rangle}\mathop{\min}_{0\leqslant i\leqslant\ell}\int_{\textnormal{U}_{N+1}}\hskip-10.00002pt\lambda_{\beta_{i}(g^{\ast}D)}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\mu(g)

\displaystyle\leqslant_{\langle X\rangle}\mathop{\min}_{0\leqslant i\leqslant\ell}\lambda_{Z_{i}}\leqslant_{\langle X\rangle}\lambda_{Z_{\mathfrak{t}}}.

On the other hand, seeing that
Z_{\mathfrak{t}}\subset Z_{\beta}(D)
for all
D\in\mathfrak{S}
, by Proposition
6.5
,
iii
), we obtain

\lambda_{Z_{\mathfrak{t}}}\leqslant_{\langle X\rangle}\int_{\textnormal{U}_{N+1}}\hskip-10.00002pt\lambda_{Z_{\beta}(g^{\ast}D)}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\mu(g).\qed

Remark 6.23
.

Likewise, (
6.12
) is reformulated as

\int_{D\in\mathfrak{S}}\hskip-5.0pt\lambda_{Z_{\beta}(D)}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\rho(D)=_{\langle X\rangle}\lambda_{Z_{\mathfrak{t}}},

where
\rho
is the unique measure on
\mathfrak{S}\cong(\mathbb{P}^{N})^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}
invariant under the unitary group
\textnormal{U}_{N+1}
and satisfying
\rho(\mathfrak{S})=1
.

Furthermore, following the same reasoning in the proofs of Theorem
6.20
and Theorem
6.22
, we generalize Theorem
6.22
further to the Grassmannian.

Theorem 6.24
.

Let
X
be a projective manifold, and choose linear systems
\mathfrak{T}
,
\mathfrak{S}_{0},\dots,\mathfrak{S}_{\ell}
, and
\mathfrak{S}
on
X
as in Theorem
6.22
.
Let
\beta_{i}
be as in Theorem
6.22
, for each
0\leqslant i\leqslant\ell
.
For any linear subsystem
\mathfrak{O}\in\Gr(k,\mathfrak{S})
, consider the map

\displaystyle Z_{k,\beta}\colon\Gr(k,\mathfrak{S})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow Z(X)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{O}

\displaystyle\longmapsto Z_{k,\beta}(\mathfrak{O})=\bigcap_{i=0}^{\ell}\beta_{i}(\mathfrak{O}),

where
\beta_{i}(\mathfrak{O})
is the subscheme of base points of
\mathfrak{O}_{\beta_{i}}
, which corresponds to
\beta_{i}(\mathbb{O})\subset\mathbb{S}_{i}
.
Then, we have

(6.14)

\int_{\textnormal{U}_{N+1}}\hskip-10.00002pt\lambda_{Z_{k,\beta}(g^{\ast}\mathfrak{O})}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\mu(g)=_{\langle X\rangle}\lambda_{Z_{\mathfrak{t}}},

where we write, by abuse of notation,
g^{\ast}\mathfrak{O}
to describe a right action
g\in\textnormal{U}_{N+1}
on
\mathfrak{O}\in\Gr(k,\mathfrak{S})
.

Remark 6.25
.

Analogous to Remark
6.21
, (
6.14
) is reformulated as

\int_{\mathfrak{O}\in\Gr(k,\mathfrak{S})}\hskip-10.00002pt\lambda_{{Z_{k,\beta}(\mathfrak{O})}}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\rho_{k,N}(\mathfrak{O})=_{\langle X\rangle}\lambda_{Z_{\mathfrak{t}}},

where
\rho_{k,N}
is the normalized measure on
\Gr(k,\mathfrak{S})\cong\Gr(k+1,N+1)^{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}
as in Remark
6.21
.
What’s more, Theorem
6.24
for
k=0
coincides with Theorem
6.22
.

Take a linear system
\mathfrak{S}\subset|L|
on
X
.
A holomorphic curve
f\colon\mathbb{C}\to X
is said to be non-degenerated relative to
\mathfrak{S}
, if the image
f(\mathbb{C})
does not lie in any
D\in\mathfrak{S}
.
By the definition of proximity functions, Crofton’s formula for Weil functions in the sense of
\mathcal{C}(X)/=_{\langle X\rangle}
implies Crofton’s formula for proximity functions modulo
O(1)
.

Theorem 6.26
.

Let
X
be a projective manifold, and choose linear systems
\mathfrak{T}
,
\mathfrak{S}_{0},\dots,\mathfrak{S}_{\ell}
, and
\mathfrak{S}
on
X
as in Theorem
6.22
.
Let
\beta_{i}
be as in Theorem
6.22
, for each
0\leqslant i\leqslant\ell
.
Let
f\colon\mathbb{C}\to X
be a holomorphic curve, which is non-degenerated relative to
\mathfrak{T}
.
If
\bigcap_{i=0}^{\ell}\textnormal{Ker}\,\beta_{i}=0
, then

\int_{D\in\mathfrak{S}}\hskip-5.0ptm_{f}(r,Z_{\beta}(D))\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\rho(D)=m_{f}(r,Z_{\mathfrak{t}})+O(1).

In addition, suppose that
\dim\left(\bigcap_{i=0}^{\ell}\textnormal{Ker}\,\beta_{i}\right)\leqslant k
, for each
0\leqslant k\leqslant N-1
. We have

\int_{\mathfrak{O}\in\Gr(k,\mathfrak{S})}\hskip-10.00002ptm_{f}(r,Z_{k,\beta}(\mathfrak{O}))\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\rho_{k,N}(\mathfrak{O})=m_{f}(r,Z_{\mathfrak{t}})+O(1).

Remark 6.27
.

The condition that
f
is non-degenerated relative to
\mathfrak{T}
and the condition that
\bigcap_{i=0}^{\ell}\textnormal{Ker}\,\beta_{i}=0
(or the condition that
\dim\left(\bigcap_{i=0}^{\ell}\textnormal{Ker}\,\beta_{i}\right)\leqslant k
, respectively) are to guarantee that
f(\mathbb{C})\not\subset Z_{\beta}(D)
(or
f(\mathbb{C})\not\subset Z_{k,\beta}(\mathfrak{O})
), and the proximity function
m_{f}(r,Z_{\beta}(D))
(or
m_{f}(r,Z_{k,\beta}(\mathfrak{O}))
) is accordingly defined for any
D\in\mathfrak{S}
(or
\mathfrak{O}\in\Gr(k,\mathfrak{S})
).
We make a convention that
m_{f}(r,Z)\equiv\infty
if
f(\mathbb{C})\subset Z
.
Even if such conditions is replaced by a weaker condition that
f
is a non-constant holomorphic curve satisfying that
f(\mathbb{C})\not\subset Z_{\mathfrak{t}}
, these two formulas in Theorem
6.26
still hold as the improper integrals.

6.5.
Calculus Lemma

Lemma 6.28

(Borel’s Calculus Lemma)
.

Let
h\colon\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}
be a monotonically increasing function.
For every
\delta>0
, the inequality

\frac{\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}}{\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}r}h(r)\leqslant h^{1+\delta}(r)

holds for all
r\in\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}
outside a Borel subset
E
of finite Lebesgue measure.

Proof.

Thanks to the monotone of
h(r)
, the derivative
\dfrac{\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}}{\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}r}h(r)
exists almost everywhere.
Observe that the inequality always holds in the particular case where
h(r)
is constant.
Assume that
h(r)\not\equiv 0
, and take
r_{0}\geqslant 0
such that
h(r_{0})>0
.
Let
E\subset\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}
be the set of
r
satisfying

\frac{\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}}{\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}r}h(r)>h^{1+\delta}(r).

Seeing that
\int_{E\cap[0,r_{0}]}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}r\leqslant r_{0}<\infty
, it suffices to show that
\int_{E\cap(r_{0},\infty)}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}r<\infty
.
By the definition of
E
, we have

\int_{E\cap(r_{0},\infty)}\!\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}r\leqslant\int_{r_{0}}^{\infty}\frac{1}{h^{1+\delta}(r)}\frac{\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}}{\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}r}h(r)\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}r\leqslant\int_{h(r_{0})}^{\infty}\frac{\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}h}{h^{1+\delta}}=\frac{1}{\delta h(r_{0})^{\delta}}<\infty.\qed

For brevity, we will use the notation

\varphi(r)\leqslant\left.\psi(r)\right\rVert_{E}

to mean that the stated inequality holds outside a Borel subset
E
of finite Lebesgue measure,
where
\varphi
and
\psi
are non-negative real-valued functions on
\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}
.

The typical use of Calculus Lemma is as follows.

Lemma 6.29
.

Let
\gamma\colon\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}
be a non-negative function on
\mathbb{C}
.
For every
\delta>0
, we have

\int_{0}^{2\pi}\gamma(re^{i\theta})\frac{\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\theta}{2\pi}\leqslant\left.\left(\mathcal{N}([\gamma\mathop{}\!\textnormal{dd}^{c}|z|^{2}],r)\right)^{(1+\delta)^{2}}\right\rVert_{E}.

Proof.

Notice that
\mathcal{N}([\gamma\mathop{}\!\textnormal{dd}^{c}|z|^{2}],r)\equiv 0
if and only if
\gamma
is almost everywhere zero on
\mathbb{C}
.
Obviously, the inequality holds in this case.
Assume that
\mathcal{N}([\gamma\mathop{}\!\textnormal{dd}^{c}|z|^{2}],r)\not\equiv 0
.
There exists a constant
r_{0}>0
such that
\mathcal{N}([\gamma\mathop{}\!\textnormal{dd}^{c}|z|^{2}],r)\geqslant\dfrac{1}{r}
for all
r\geqslant r_{0}
.
Using the polar coordinates, we see that
\mathop{}\!\textnormal{dd}^{c}|z|^{2}=2r\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}r\wedge\dfrac{\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\theta}{2\pi}
.
Hence

r\frac{\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}}{\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}r}\mathcal{N}([\gamma\mathop{}\!\textnormal{dd}^{c}|z|^{2}],r)=\int_{\bigtriangleup(r)}\gamma(z)\mathop{}\!\textnormal{dd}^{c}|z|^{2}=\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\theta}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{r}2t\gamma(te^{i\theta})\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}t.

Applying the Borel’s Calculus Lemma twice, we get

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{2\pi}\gamma(re^{i\theta})\frac{\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\theta}{2\pi}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2r}\frac{\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}}{\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}r}\left(r\frac{\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}}{\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}r}\mathcal{N}([\gamma\mathop{}\!\textnormal{dd}^{c}|z|^{2}],r)\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{1}{r}\frac{\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}}{\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}r}\mathcal{N}([\gamma\mathop{}\!\textnormal{dd}^{c}|z|^{2}],r)+\frac{\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}^{2}}{\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}r^{2}}\mathcal{N}([\gamma\mathop{}\!\textnormal{dd}^{c}|z|^{2}],r)\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\left.\left(\frac{1}{r}+\left(\mathcal{N}([\gamma\mathop{}\!\textnormal{dd}^{c}|z|^{2}],r)\right)^{\delta(1+\delta)}\right)\left(\mathcal{N}([\gamma\mathop{}\!\textnormal{dd}^{c}|z|^{2}],r)\right)^{1+\delta}\right\rVert_{E_{1}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\left.\left(\mathcal{N}([\gamma\mathop{}\!\textnormal{dd}^{c}|z|^{2}],r)\right)^{(1+\delta)^{2}}\right\rVert_{E_{1}\cup[0,r_{0}]}.\qed

Remark 6.30
.

Lemma
6.29
refines the typical application of Calculus Lemma (such as Lemma 3.4 in
[
LC90
]
, or Lemma A​ 2.1.5 in
[
Ru01
]
), from the original Borel’s Calculus Lemma rather than the improved version of Calculus Lemma notwithstanding.

For any line bundle
L
and any ample line bundle
A
on
X
, one can find a positive integer
m
such that
L^{-1}\otimes A^{m}
is ample.
Consequently, we immediately obtain the following property of the characteristic functions.

Proposition 6.31
.

Let
L
and
A
be as above. Then there is a constant
c
such that for any non-constant holomorphic curve
f\colon\mathbb{C}\to X
,

T_{f}(r,L)\leqslant c\,T_{f}(r,A).

Moreover, if
L
is ample as well, then there is a constant
c
such that for any non-constant holomorphic curve
f\colon\mathbb{C}\to X
,

(6.15)

\frac{1}{c}\,T_{f}(r,A)\leqslant T_{f}(r,L)\leqslant c\,T_{f}(r,A).

Definition 6.32
.

Let
S_{f}(r)
represent any quantity
\psi\colon\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}
satisfying that

\psi(r)\leqslant\left.O(\log T_{f}(r,A))\right\rVert_{E}.

Especially, if
f
is an algebraic curve,

\psi(r)\leqslant O(1)\quad(r\to\infty).

That is to say, there exists a constant
c
such that

\psi(r)\leqslant c,\qquad\forall\,r\geqslant r_{0},

for some constant
r_{0}>0
.

We see from (
6.15
) that the notation
S_{f}(r)
is independent of the choice of the ample line bundle
A
.

7.
Ahlfors’ lemma on logarithmic derivative

7.1.
Derived curves or associated curves

First, recall the
k
-th derived curve (or associated curve) of a holomorphic curve
f
(see Section 2.1 of
[
Fuj93
]
or Chapter 2, Section 4.1 of
[
GH78
]
for more details).
Let
f
be a holomorphic curve from
\mathbb{C}
to
\mathbb{P}^{n}
, and take a reduced representation
[f_{0}:\dots:f_{n}]
of
f
.
Thereupon one gets a holomorphic curve

\displaystyle F\colon\mathbb{C}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}^{n+1}\setminus\{0\}

\displaystyle z

\displaystyle\longmapsto(f_{0}(z),\dots,f_{n}(z)).

It is apparent that
f=\pr^{n}\circ F
, where
\pr^{n}\colon\mathbb{C}^{n+1}\setminus\{0\}\to\mathbb{P}^{n}
is the canonical projection through the origin
0\in\mathbb{C}^{n+1}
.
For each
0\leqslant k\leqslant n
, consider the holomorphic curve

(7.1)

\displaystyle F^{k}\colon\mathbb{C}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\bigwedge^{k+1}\mathbb{C}^{n+1}

\displaystyle z

\displaystyle\longmapsto F(z)\wedge F^{(1)}(z)\wedge\dots\wedge F^{(k)}(z),

where
F^{(\ell)}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=(f_{0}^{(\ell)},\dots,f_{n}^{(\ell)})
is a holomorphic curve in
\mathbb{C}^{n+1}
, for each
1\leqslant\ell\leqslant k
.
In particular,
F^{n}
is indeed a holomorphic function, known as the Wronskian
W(f)
of
f
.

A holomorphic curve
f
in
\mathbb{P}^{n}
is said to be linear non-degenerate, if its image does not lie in any hyperplane of
\mathbb{P}^{n}
.
It is evident that
F^{k}\equiv 0
, if and only if the image
f(\mathbb{C})
lies in a linear subspace
\mathbb{P}^{k-1}\subset\mathbb{P}^{n}
.

One abusively denotes by
\pr\colon\bigwedge^{k+1}\mathbb{C}^{n+1}\setminus\{0\}\to\textnormal{P}(\bigwedge^{k+1}\mathbb{C}^{n+1})\cong\mathbb{P}^{n_{k}}
the canonical projection through the origin
0\in\mathbb{C}^{n_{k}+1}\cong\bigwedge^{k+1}\mathbb{C}^{n+1}
, where
n_{k}=\binom{n+1}{k+1}-1
.
Assume that the image
f(\mathbb{C})
does not lie in any linear subspace
\mathbb{P}^{k-1}\subset\mathbb{P}^{n}
.
Then one defines a holomorphic curve

f^{k}\colon\mathbb{C}\longrightarrow\textnormal{P}(\bigwedge^{k+1}\mathbb{C}^{n+1})\cong\mathbb{P}^{n_{k}},

to be the analytic extension of
\pr\circ F_{\restriction U}^{k}
, where
U=\mathbb{C}\setminus(F^{k})^{-1}(0)
.
Take the blow-up of
\mathbb{C}^{n_{k}+1}
at the origin

\upsilon_{0}\colon\widetilde{\mathbb{C}}^{n_{k}+1}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\Bl_{\mathcal{I}_{0}}(\mathbb{C}^{n_{k}+1})\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}^{n_{k}+1},

where
\mathcal{I}_{0}=\{s\in\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{C}^{n_{k}+1}}\mid s(0)=0\}
.
It is evident that the rational map
\mathbb{C}^{n_{k}+1}\dashrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{n_{k}}
is resolved into
\widetilde{\pr}\colon\widetilde{\mathbb{C}}^{n_{k}+1}\to\mathbb{P}^{n_{k}}
by the blow-up
\upsilon_{0}
.
If one denotes the lifting of
F^{k}
to
\widetilde{\mathbb{C}}^{n_{k}+1}
by
\widetilde{F}^{k}
, then
f^{k}=\widetilde{\pr}\circ\widetilde{F}^{k}
, which is called the
k
-th derived curve of
f
by Fujimoto
[
Fuj93
]
.

One says that
\bm{v}\in\bigwedge^{k+1}\mathbb{C}^{n+1}
is a decomposable
(k+1)
-vector if it can be written as
\bm{v}=v_{0}\wedge\dots\wedge v_{k}
with
k+1
vectors
v_{i}\in\mathbb{C}^{n+1}
for
0\leqslant i\leqslant k
.
Observe that every non-zero decomposable
(k+1)
-vector
\bm{v}\in\bigwedge^{k+1}\mathbb{C}^{n+1}
determines an element
S
in
\Gr(k+1,n+1)
,
and
\pr(\bm{v})=\Pluc(S)
, where one abusively writes the Plücker embedding
\Pluc\colon\Gr(k+1,n+1)\hookrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{n_{k}}
.
By definition, all the image of
F_{k}
are decomposable, thus
f^{k}
factors through the Plücker embedding into a holomorphic curve
f_{k}
in the Grassmannian, namely,

(7.2)

f^{k}=\Pluc\circ f_{k}.

Thinking of
\Gr(k+1,n+1)
as a submanifold of
\textnormal{P}(\bigwedge^{k+1}\mathbb{C}^{n+1})
, Griffiths and Harris
[
GH78
]
regard
f^{k}
and
f_{k}
as one holomorphic curve, which is referred to as the
k
-th associated curve of
f
.

Let
\omega_{k}=\mathop{}\!\textnormal{dd}^{c}\log\|x\|^{2}
be the Kähler form associated to the Fubini-Study metric on
\mathbb{P}^{n_{k}}
, where
\|x\|^{2}=\sum_{i=0}^{n_{k}}|x_{i}|^{2}
for a reduced representation
[x_{0}:\dots:x_{n_{k}}]
of
x\in\mathbb{P}^{n_{k}}
.
By calculation, one obtains the infinitesimal Plücker formula (see Chapter 2, Section 4.3 of
[
GH78
]
for details)

(7.3)

(f^{k})^{\ast}\omega_{k}=\frac{|F^{k-1}|^{2}|F^{k+1}|^{2}}{|F^{k}|^{4}}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{dd}^{c}|z|^{2},

for
z\in\mathbb{C}
, where we make a convention that
F^{-1}=1
.
One writes
h_{k}=\dfrac{|F^{k-1}|^{2}|F^{k+1}|^{2}}{|F^{k}|^{4}}
and
\Omega_{k}=(f^{k})^{\ast}\omega_{k}
.
Hence it follows from the Poincaré-Lelong formula that

\mathop{}\!\textnormal{dd}^{c}[\log h_{k}]=[\Omega_{k-1}]+[\Omega_{k+1}]-2[\Omega_{k}]+\frac{1}{2}[Z_{h_{k}}],

where
Z_{h_{k}}
is the closed analytic subspace of
\mathbb{C}
defined by the equation
h_{k}=0
.
Given a point
z_{0}\in\mathbb{C}
, by choosing a suitable reduced representation of
f
, one writes
f
in normal form near
z_{0}

f(z)=[1+\dots:(z-z_{0})^{\nu_{1}+1}+\dots:(z-z_{0})^{\nu_{1}+\nu_{2}+2}+\dots:\dots:(z-z_{0})^{\nu_{1}+\dots+\nu_{n}+n}+\cdots],

with
\nu_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
for all
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
.
One easily checks that
F^{k}
has a zero of order
k\nu_{1}+(k-1)\nu_{2}+\dots+\nu_{k}
at
z_{0}
.
Here
F^{k}
is said to have a zero of order
0
at
z_{0}
, if
F^{k}(z_{0})\neq 0
.
Therefore,
h_{k}
has a zero of order
2\nu_{k+1}
at
z_{0}
.

7.2.
Ahlfors’ lemma on logarithmic derivative in projective spaces

In this subsection, we are going to recall Ahlfors’ Lemma on logarithmic derivative (Ahlfors’ LLD for short) for the hyperplanes of
\mathbb{P}^{n}
.

Let us briefly introduce the
k
-th contact function of
f
(see Section 2.3 of
[
Fuj93
]
or Section A​ 3.5 of
[
Ru01
]
for more details).
